3. Month-end invoice generation for all active assets.

The real need is now clearer: this is not just a calculator or reporting script. It is meant to become a controlled operating layer for a leasing company. The scripts need to support manual operation first, preserve traceability and recovery, train named users on a sample workflow, then lock a clean baseline and support a monitored production period before eventual handoff to an operations agent.

What the system needs to do, in business terms, is this: ALC draws on a revolving bank line to acquire assets, assigns those assets to properties, leases them out, invoices the lease installments, tracks the related bank financing cost, and maintains an auditable history of assets, invoices, balances, and closed periods. Each asset should also have its own Excel-based calculation unit created at intake, then updated over time as actual invoices are posted and rates change. The outputs need to support three books at once: customer invoicing history, bank financing history, and asset inventory status.

What you have so far is a strong first operational prototype. The intent is already captured well in [ALC/ALC_operating_manual.md](ALC/ALC_operating_manual.md) and implemented in broad form in [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py). The current design already includes the right control concepts: baseline config, posted invoice ledger, closed periods, run manifests, backups, daily reporting, month-end invoicing, and bank-payable summaries. The wrapper commands in [ALC/scripts](ALC/scripts) also fit the intended operator workflow. So the foundation is correct.

What is still incomplete is the operating model around the asset-level workbook and the production-quality data flow. Right now the system behaves more like a portfolio engine than a per-asset dossier system. The workbook output exists, but it is still portfolio-oriented rather than “create one Excel tab immediately when a new asset is entered, then keep that tab updated with actual invoice history and revised rates.” Also, the live working files are not yet in a clean production-ready state: [ALC/assets.csv](ALC/assets.csv) does not currently look like a reliable operating master, and the expected live ledger/control files are not yet present in the working set. That means the architecture is pointing in the right direction, but the real operating lifecycle you described is only partially implemented.

So the best way to define “what we have hitherto” is: a compliant prototype for lease tracking and month-end control, with auditability concepts already in place, but not yet fully shaped into the exact asset-bookkeeping workflow needed for training, baseline certification, short supervised production, and handoff.

The next steps should be sequenced, not mixed:

1. Define the target operating lifecycle precisely.
This should lock the stages: training mode with sample files, fine-tune mode, baseline initialization, supervised production, and final handoff. That lifecycle should become the top-level design, not just an implied process.

2. Separate master records from generated operational artifacts.
You need one clean master asset register, one rates table, one posted invoice ledger, one closed-period register, and one per-asset calculation workbook or workbook tab policy. Right now that last part needs stronger definition.

3. Implement asset-intake automation.
When a new asset is added to the asset master, the system should automatically create or update the Excel calculation unit for that asset. That is a core requirement from your description and should be treated as first-class functionality, not an optional report.

4. Make invoice posting update both the ledger and the asset calculation unit.
Each invoice run should not only write to the posted ledger, but also update the relevant asset tab with actual installment, actual rate used, interest, amortization, and ending balance.

5. Define the official output package.
At minimum this should include:
customer invoice history,
bank financing and payable history,
active asset inventory,
projected and posted amortization by asset,
closed-period evidence,
run manifests and backups.

Andres and Ana should get a constrained sample environment with seeded files, scripted walkthroughs, and expected outputs. Training should not happen against loosely prepared production files.

7. Run a formal baseline only after workflow signoff.
Baseline should mean: approved file structure, approved sample-to-production transition, approved output pack, approved exception handling, and confirmed restore procedure.

8. Run supervised production for a few months.
That period should validate whether the process is stable enough for an operations agent to own it without depending on you.

[user.message]
explain closed periods as input

[assistant.message]
`closed_periods.csv` is an input in the sense that the script reads it as a control file before doing anything that could change history. It is not a business input like assets or rates; it is an operational control input.

What it does:
It tells the system which months are already closed and therefore must be treated as locked. If a month is listed there, the script should not allow invoice rows for that month to be overwritten or recalculated as if they were still open.

Example:
If `2026-07` is in `closed_periods.csv`, then July 2026 is closed.
That means:
1. July invoice history is frozen.
2. July schedule results used as actuals remain fixed.
3. Later rate changes cannot rewrite July.
4. Corrections should be posted as adjustments in a later open month, unless an authorized override exists.

Why it is needed:
Without a closed-period control, an operator or future agent could rerun the script after a rate update or asset edit and accidentally change historical outputs. That would break auditability.

Why I called it an input:
Because the script needs to read it on every run to know what is editable and what is not. In that sense it is a system input, even though the file is mostly system-maintained.

A better way to think about the file categories is:

1. Business inputs
Files the operator maintains for the business itself.
Example:
`assets.xlsx`
`rates.csv`

2. Control inputs
Files the system reads to enforce rules.
Example:
`closed_periods.csv`

3. System outputs
Files the system generates as records or reports.
Example:
posted invoice ledger,
bank payable reports,
daily reports,
asset workbook updates.

So if you prefer clearer terminology, we can stop calling `closed_periods.csv` a normal input and instead define it as:
an official control file read by the system and updated only during month close.

My recommendation:
Keep `closed_periods.csv`, but classify it as a control file, not a normal manual input.

If that works for you, I’ll restate item 2 as:
1. Manual business inputs: `assets.xlsx`, `rates.csv`
2. Control file: `closed_periods.csv`

And then move to the next question.

[user.message]
why excel? this will all be operated by an agent when all is fine-tuned

[assistant.message]
Excel only makes sense if you want humans to directly operate and inspect the main asset book during training and early manual use. If the long-term owner is an agent and not a human operator, then Excel should not be the system of record by default.

Why I suggested Excel:
1. You said the asset file should have an inventory tab plus one tab per asset.
2. You referenced the asset calculation unit as an Excel-style workbook.
3. It is convenient for Andres and Ana during tutorial and test-run.

But those are usability reasons, not architecture reasons.

If the real target is agent-operated production, then the cleaner design is:

1. Structured data files as the source of truth
Examples:
`assets.csv`
`rates.csv`
`posted_invoices.csv`
`closed_periods.csv`

2. Generated human-readable workbook as an output
Example:
`ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx`
This workbook is produced by the script for review, training, and handoff, but it is not the primary system record.

That approach is better for an agent because:
1. CSV/JSON are easier and safer to read, validate, diff, repair, and regenerate.
2. They reduce the risk of manual corruption inside a workbook.
3. The agent can rebuild Excel outputs whenever needed from clean source data.
4. Auditability is stronger when the official history lives in flat ledgers and control files.

So the real question is not “Excel or not,” but this:

Should Excel be:
1. The main operational source file humans and the agent both update.
2. A generated reporting workbook built from structured source files.

My recommendation, given your latest point:
Use structured files as the official source of truth, and generate Excel as an output artifact.

That means:
1. `assets.csv` or another structured master file remains the asset source.
2. The script generates `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx` with:
an `Inventory` tab,
one tab per asset,
history and lifetime estimate per asset.
3. Invoice posting updates the source ledgers first, then regenerates or updates the workbook output.

This is more robust for agent operation.

So I would revise the earlier suggestion:
- Keep the old file-map reasoning.
- Do not force `assets.xlsx` as the master input.
- Treat the Excel workbook as an operational output unless you explicitly want humans editing it.

Question for you:
Do you want the source of truth for assets to remain a structured file like `assets.csv`, with the Excel workbook generated from it?

[user.message]
good, it makes sense. Question: when a monthly run finds that the last invoice for the asset is issued, therefore leaving the asset inactive and ready to recover/salvage, will this update the inventory file? is is automated?

[assistant.message]
I’m checking the month-end and snapshot logic directly so I can tell you whether deactivation on the last invoice is currently automatic or still missing.

[assistant.message]
Not fully automated today.

What happens now:
The inventory status in the generated workbook is derived from the calculated balance, not manually typed into the workbook. In [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py), `build_snapshot` marks an asset as inactive when its remaining balance is $0$ or less, provided the asset had been marked active in the source asset file. That means the workbook can reflect “inactive” automatically once the last installment is posted.

What does not happen automatically today:
The monthly `invoice` run in [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py) updates the posted invoice ledger, but it does not regenerate the Excel Asset Calculation Unit as part of that same command. So after the final invoice is posted, the workbook will only show the updated inactive status when the `one-pager` workbook is run again. Also, there is no separate automated lifecycle state like “ready for recovery/salvage”; the current logic only flips between `active` and `inactive`.

So the answer is:
1. The status logic is automatic in the workbook generation.
2. The workbook refresh after invoice posting is not yet automatic.
3. A distinct post-lease state such as `ready_for_salvage` is not yet implemented.

If you want, the next improvement should be straightforward:
1. Make the `invoice` command automatically rebuild the Excel workbook after posting invoices.
2. Add a clearer lifecycle field so inventory can show `active`, `completed`, `ready_for_salvage`, `returned`, or `decommissioned` instead of only `active`/`inactive`.

[user.message]
sounds good, but before that, how are the changes mades on the next month? how?

[assistant.message]
Great point. Next-month changes happen through a simple roll-forward model:

1. Closed month stays frozen  
If July is closed, July rows are locked and cannot be recomputed.

2. New month uses latest known inputs  
When you run August invoicing, the script reads:
- current assets file
- current rates file
- posted invoice ledger (historical actuals)

3. History anchors the new calculation  
For each asset, August starts from the last posted ending balance, then computes the new installment logic for open periods using the applicable rate for August.

4. Rate changes affect open/future periods only  
If rate changed effective August, July remains unchanged, and August onward reflects the new rate.

5. Asset changes apply from the moment they are effective  
If you add a new asset or change status before August run, August outputs include that state.

So “how changes are made next month” in practice:

1. Update assets file if needed.  
2. Update rates file if there is a new effective rate.  
3. Run monthly invoice for the new month.  
4. Script posts August rows, refreshes workbook, and (if requested) closes August.

That is exactly why the model uses posted ledger + closed periods: freeze the past, roll forward the future.

[user.message]
when does the administrator approval kicks in?

[assistant.message]
In the current script design, administrator approval kicks in only at exception points, not in normal monthly flow.

Right now, that means:

1. Normal case (no admin needed)  
Open month invoicing and month close run as standard operations.

2. Admin approval required  
When someone tries to touch a month already marked closed, approval is represented by running invoice with the override option in [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py).  
That is the technical “approval gate” today.

So practically:
1. Close month normally.
2. If correction is needed later, default path is adjustment in current open month.
3. Only if a true exception is approved, use closed-month override.

Important KISS note:
There is no built-in user-role authentication yet (no login/permission engine). “Admin approval” is currently a controlled process decision plus the explicit override flag usage and audit trail (manifest + backup), not identity-based access control.

If you want, next step can be a very simple approval workflow without adding heavy auth:
1. Add a required reason field for closed-month override.
2. Add required approved_by field.
3. Store both in the run manifest so every override is auditable.

[assistant.message]
Here is the story of how the model works today, and what is still pending before real testing.

**The Story**
Think of the system as a small operating engine for a leasing company.

Each asset starts life when it is entered in [ALC/assets.csv](ALC/assets.csv). That start date is not just descriptive. It becomes the anchor for that asset’s lease life. From that date, the model builds the expected installment schedule one month at a time, keeping the same day-of-month when possible. So if an asset activates on the 23rd, the expected due dates follow that anniversary pattern.

The pricing side comes from [ALC/rates.csv](ALC/rates.csv). When rates change, the model does not rewrite closed history. Instead, it recalculates only the open and future part of each asset’s financial life. That means every asset has two realities at once: locked past actuals and adjustable future projections.

Every day, the operator runs [ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh](ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh). That script reads the current assets, rates, and posted history, then answers: what is the portfolio status today, what is coming due, and is invoice day near? It now also understands a monthly billing day, defaulting to the 22nd, and if that falls on a weekend it shifts to the next working day. The daily run is the monitoring layer. It does not post accounting history, but it tells the operator what is approaching.

On invoice day, the operator runs [ALC/scripts/op_invoice.sh](ALC/scripts/op_invoice.sh). That command looks at the billing window between the previous billing run date and the current billing run date, finds the asset installments due in that window, and posts them into the invoice ledger. At that point, those rows become historical actuals. The same run also refreshes the Excel workbook [ALC/ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx](ALC/ALC%20-%20Asset%20Calculation%20Unit.xlsx), so the Inventory tab and every asset tab reflect the newly posted state.

At month-end, the operator runs [ALC/scripts/op_month_end.sh](ALC/scripts/op_month_end.sh). That script does two things: it records the bank payable summary for that month, and then it closes the month. Once the month is closed, it is treated as locked. Future reruns cannot silently recalculate it.

So the model today is built around a simple discipline:
1. Update assets when assets change.
2. Update rates when rates change.
3. Run daily every day.
4. Run invoice on billing day.
5. Run month-end to record bank payable and lock the month.

**What Exists Now**
The core engine is in [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py). It currently supports:
1. Asset-level schedules with anniversary-based due dates.
2. Batch billing date logic with next-working-day adjustment.
3. Daily reminders for invoice day minus one and invoice day itself.
4. Posted invoice history with due date and billing date fields.
5. Workbook refresh after invoice posting.
6. Inventory-first Excel workbook with one tab per asset.
7. Month closing through the closed-period mechanism.
8. One bank payable row per month in a CSV when bank-payable is run.

Operational wrappers now exist for the 3-script model:
1. [ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh](ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh)
2. [ALC/scripts/op_invoice.sh](ALC/scripts/op_invoice.sh)
3. [ALC/scripts/op_month_end.sh](ALC/scripts/op_month_end.sh)

**What Is Still Pending Before Testing**
What remains is mostly packaging, cleanup, and test-readiness, not major logic.

1. File set cleanup.
The folder still contains training/baseline/test artifacts such as [ALC/assets.sample.csv](ALC/assets.sample.csv), [ALC/posted_invoices.test.csv](ALC/posted_invoices.test.csv), [ALC/posted_invoices.baseline.csv](ALC/posted_invoices.baseline.csv), and [ALC/closed_periods.baseline.csv](ALC/closed_periods.baseline.csv). That is fine for development, but before operator testing you likely want one clean official file set with final names.

2. Live file initialization.
The current visible official files are not yet fully aligned with the intended production names. For example, [ALC/posted_invoices.csv](ALC/posted_invoices.csv), [ALC/closed_periods.csv](ALC/closed_periods.csv), [ALC/baseline_config.json](ALC/baseline_config.json), and [ALC/bank_payable.csv](ALC/bank_payable.csv) are conceptually part of the operating model, but not all of them exist yet in the clean final form.

3. Documentation alignment.
The operating model changed materially, but [ALC/ALC_operating_manual.md](ALC/ALC_operating_manual.md) and the training/reference materials have not yet been updated to reflect:
- the 3-script workflow
- anniversary-based due dates
- billing-day logic
- bank payable monthly CSV
- month-end close as a separate step

4. One end-to-end operator test pack.
Before Andres and Ana test, you should have one clean scenario with:
- sample assets
- sample rate change
- one daily run
- one invoice-day run
- one month-end run
- expected terminal outputs and resulting files

5. Optional but useful final review.
The system now supports bank payable CSV logic, but I have not seen a real final [ALC/bank_payable.csv](ALC/bank_payable.csv) in the workspace yet because it is generated on run. That is normal, but it means the first official test run should intentionally create it and confirm the location and naming are exactly what you want.

**Short Version**
The model today works like this: assets and rates are the only human-maintained business inputs; daily run monitors; invoice-day run posts asset-level installments and refreshes the workbook; month-end records bank payable and locks the month. What is still pending before testing is mostly cleanup of the file surface, final naming, and updating the training/manual materials to match the actual behavior.

Tomorrow’s work is probably this:
1. Finalize the clean operator file structure.
2. Rename/remove baseline and test artifacts from the operator surface.
3. Update the manual, reference card, and training deck.
4. Run one formal end-to-end test scenario.

**Class Goal**
Teach Ana/Andres to run ALC safely from baseline setup through daily use and month-end close, understanding every file they touch and every file the system creates.

Non-technical operators who can edit CSV and run terminal commands.

4. “At month-end we issue invoices and close the period.”
5. “System preserves traceability via ledger, manifests, and backups.”
6. “If something changes later, we don’t rewrite history; we use controlled adjustment flow.”

Use this message throughout:
- Inputs are controlled by users.
- Outputs are generated by system.
- Posted ledger is history.

---

**Learning Objectives**
By the end, users can:
1. Identify all core files and their purpose.

- Why baseline exists
- What reset flags do
- What gets cleared, what doesn’t

4. Guided test run (30 min)
- Run baseline
- Run daily
- Run schedule
- Run invoice (with close-period)
- Run bank-payable
- Run one-pager

5. Validate outputs (15 min)
- Check invoice CSV
- Check posted ledger updated
- Check closed month written
- Check manifest created
- Check backups created
- Check one-pager tabs

6. Troubleshooting and Q&A (10 min)
- Missing file path
- Wrong date format
- Closed month errors
- Go-live block errors

Use wrapper scripts only.

- ledger reset
- closed periods reset
- manifest created

Expected:
- payable total
- manifest created

6. One-pager workbook
- `.op_one_pager.sh german`

Expected:
- workbook updated
- one tab per asset
- manifest created

**Validation Checklist (give as handout)**
After each cycle confirm:

1. Inputs valid
- assets.csv: no `$`, `%`, commas in numeric cells
- rates.csv: date format accepted (`YYYY-MM-DD` preferred)

2. Controls updated
- ALC/posted_invoices.csv has new posted rows
- ALC/closed_periods.csv includes closed month

3. Outputs exist
- ALC/invoices_YYYY-MM.csv
- ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx

4. Audit trail exists
- new JSON in run_manifests
- backup folder in backups

---

**Common Errors to Teach**
1. Running inside `>>>` Python prompt
- Fix: exit and use shell terminal

2. Missing subcommand
- Fix: use `daily`, `invoice`, etc.

3. Missing rates/assets file
- Fix: verify exact file path

4. Date parse errors
- Fix: normalize date formats in CSV

5. “Month is closed”
- Meaning: control worked
- Fix: only admins run authorized adjustment route

6. “Before go-live”
- Fix: align month with baseline go-live

---

**Roles and Guardrails**
1. Operator
- updates inputs
- runs wrappers
- validates outputs

2. Admin
- can run full CLI with overrides
- handles adjustments/reopens
- manages structure changes

Guardrail:
- Never edit posted ledger by hand unless under formal correction procedure.

---

**Class Close (what success looks like)**
1. User can run full cycle with wrappers only.
2. User can explain each core file.
3. User can validate outputs without developer help.
4. User can identify where audit evidence is stored.

**Class Goal**  
Teach Ana and Andres to run ALC safely from baseline setup through daily monitoring, invoice-day posting, and month-end close, understanding which files they edit, which files the system updates, and where history is preserved.

**Audience**  
Non-technical operators who can edit CSV files and run terminal commands.

**Format**
1. 90 minutes live
2. 3 parts:
- Storyline and concepts
- Guided walkthrough
- Hands-on test run and validation

---

**Storyline (business narrative)**
1. “We receive or update assets and bank rates.”
2. “Each asset gets its own lease life based on its activation date.”
3. “System calculates expected installments across the asset’s lifespan.”

6. “At month-end we record bank payable and close the month.”
7. “System preserves traceability through posted ledger, closed periods, manifests, and backups.”
8. “If something changes later, we do not rewrite closed history.”

Use this message throughout:

- Posted ledger is history.
- Closed periods protect history.

- Month-end closes.

Teach the workflow as three recurring operational steps plus one one-time setup.

- baseline initialization

2. Every day
- run daily script
- review portfolio totals
- review upcoming invoice reminder
- review any changes from updated assets or rates

3. On invoice day
- run invoice script
- post invoice rows for all installments due in the billing window
- refresh workbook automatically

4. At month-end
- run month-end script
- update bank payable monthly CSV
- close the month

---

**Key Concept Slide: Due Date vs Billing Date**
This should be a dedicated slide.

1. Asset due date
- each asset has its own installment due date
- based on activation/start date anniversary
- example: asset started on the 23rd, due dates follow the 23rd pattern

2. Billing date
- one monthly batch run date for invoicing
- default example: 22nd of the month
- if weekend, shifts to next working day

3. Month close
- separate control step
- locks the month after bank payable is recorded

Message to teach:
- Due dates belong to assets.
- Billing date belongs to the monthly invoicing process.
- Month close belongs to controls and auditability.

- `run_manifests/`
- `backups/`

- `op_init_baseline.sh`
- `op_daily.sh`

- `op_month_end.sh`

- `ALC_operating_manual.pdf`

---

**File Behavior to Teach**

1. `assets.csv`
- operator adds or updates assets here
- start date is the activation date anchor
- system uses this to build each asset’s lease path

2. `rates.csv`
- operator adds new bank rates when they change
- system applies new rates to open and future periods only

3. `posted_invoices.csv`
- official historical invoice ledger
- do not edit manually
- once posted and closed, this is locked history

4. `closed_periods.csv`
- system control file
- marks closed months
- do not edit manually during normal operation

5. `bank_payable.csv`
- one row per month
- updated by month-end process
- operator can review it, but should not manually maintain it

6. `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx`
- `Inventory` first tab
- one tab per asset after that
- system-generated operational workbook
- reflects active/inactive status and schedule state

explain this 1. Use a dedicated training folder copy so production files are untouched.

[assistant.message]
It means: do not train directly in the live production folder.

Why:
1. Training runs create real side effects:
- updates to `posted_invoices.csv`
- updates to `closed_periods.csv`
- updates to `bank_payable.csv`
- new manifests and backups
- workbook refreshes
2. If done in production paths, those test actions look like real operations and pollute history.

How to apply it:
1. Make a separate folder (a clone of the ALC structure) just for training.
2. Run all training commands there.
3. Keep production folder for real activity only.

Practical example:
1. `ALC/` = production
2. `ALC_training/` = training sandbox

Then in training:
1. baseline reset
2. add fictional assets/rates
3. run daily/invoice/month-end
4. validate behavior

Result:
1. trainees can practice safely
2. no risk of corrupting or contaminating production records
3. production baseline remains clean and auditable.

--- slide3 ---

BUSINESS NARRATIVE The Storyline 1 We receive or update assets and bank rates. 2 System calculates lease behavior month by month. 3 Daily we monitor the portfolio. 4 At month-end we issue invoices and close the period. 5 System preserves traceability via ledger, manifests, and backups. 6 If something changes later, we don't rewrite history — we use the controlled adjustment flow. Inputs are controlled by users. Outputs are generated by the system. The posted ledger is history. ALC OPERATIONS TRAINING 03

**ALC Operator Reference Card**

*Zero-baseline operating model · keep at your desk*

**WHAT DAILY RUN TELLS YOU**

**1. Daily Monitoring**

op_daily.sh <operator> <as-of YYYY-MM-DD> [billing_day]

op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22

**2. Invoice Day**

op_invoice.sh <operator> <month YYYY-MM> [billing_day]

op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22

**3. Month-End**

op_month_end.sh <operator> <month YYYY-MM> [billing_day]

op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22

**ONE-TIME SETUP — BASELINE**

– Current portfolio totals

– Billing month

– Billing date

– Invoice lines due

– Invoice amount due

– Reminder if invoice day is tomorrow or today

**WHAT INVOICE-DAY RUN DOES**

– Posts invoice rows into posted_invoices.csv

– Creates invoices_YYYY-MM.csv

– Refreshes ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx

– Creates manifest and backups

**WHAT MONTH-END RUN DOES**

– Updates bank_payable.csv

– Stores one row per month

– Closes the month in closed_periods.csv

– Creates manifests and backups

**IMPORTANT CONCEPTS**

op_init_baseline.sh <operator> <go-live YYYY-MM-DD>

<notes>

**FILES YOU EDIT**

– assets.csv

– rates.csv

**FILES YOU REVIEW — NEVER EDIT**

**Due Date**

Each asset has its own due date pattern based on its start date.

**Billing Date**

One monthly batch invoicing date. Default example: 22. Weekend

shifts to next working day.

Separate control step. Once closed, the month is locked.

– posted_invoices.csv

- invoice day

[user.message]
I dont understand these rouitine commands, is the name of the operator needed? can any name be used? whey all billing day dates are needed in all moments ? what does that date mean?

[assistant.message]
Great questions. You are spotting exactly the parts that confuse operators first time.

1. Is operator name required?
Yes, in these wrapper scripts it is required as the first argument.
It is used for traceability in manifests and output logs.

2. Can any name be used?
Yes, any consistent identifier can be used.
Examples: andres, ana, german, team1.
Best practice:
use one stable name per person so audit history is clear.

3. Is billing_day required every time?
No. It is optional in daily, invoice, and month-end wrappers.
If omitted, the script uses the default billing day 22.

So both are valid:
- op_daily.sh andres 2026-07-21
- op_daily.sh andres 2026-07-21 22

4. Why does billing day appear in multiple commands?
Because each command uses it for a different purpose:
- Daily: calculates reminder timing and shows current billing date.
- Invoice day: determines which due dates belong in this billing batch.
- Month-end: keeps bank payable and close logic aligned to the same billing cycle.

5. What does billing day actually mean?
It is the monthly portfolio batch invoicing anchor date.
Example with 22:
- Billing date target is day 22 each month.
- If day 22 is a weekend, system shifts to next working day.
- Asset due dates remain per asset; billing day is the shared batch posting date.

Quick practical rule for class:
1. Always include operator name.
2. You may omit billing day if you use 22 policy.
3. Include billing day explicitly during training to avoid ambiguity.

[user.message]
so, does it mean that if I plan one month to invoice on the 23, I can just start running daily, invoice and month-end commands for that month typing 23 instead of 22?

[assistant.message]
Yes, exactly.

If you want that month (or your operating policy) to bill on day 23, run with `23` as billing day in all three routine commands for consistency:

1. Daily
- `op_daily.sh <operator> <as-of YYYY-MM-DD> 23`

2. Invoice day
- `op_invoice.sh <operator> <month YYYY-MM> 23`

3. Month-end
- `op_month_end.sh <operator> <month YYYY-MM> 23`

Important:
1. Use the same billing day across daily/invoice/month-end for the same cycle.
2. If day 23 falls on weekend, system shifts to next working day automatically.
3. Asset due dates still come from each asset start date; billing day only defines the shared batch posting anchor.

[user.message]
1. so, daily wrapper date changes every day according to as-of date, right?

[assistant.message]
Yes, correct.

`op_daily.sh` uses the as-of date as “today’s evaluation date,” so it should change each day you run it.

Example:
1. Today: `op_daily.sh andres 2026-07-06 23`
2. Tomorrow: `op_daily.sh andres 2026-07-07 23`

So only the `as-of` date moves daily; billing day usually stays fixed unless policy changes.

[user.message]
Questions:

[assistant.message]
Great, send them.  
Number them `1, 2, 3...` and I’ll answer each one clearly and quickly.

[user.message]
1. To test-run invoicing script, do I need to wait until the 22th? or can I run that script on any day and it will invoice from the previos run to date? 2. Should I enter on the assets sheet three with previous dates, then on next day run invoices and then month end and how do I know it will work? 3. if I do that (invoice on the 10th for instance), assets with due dates from 11th to the 30th of that month will acumulate to next month? or can invoices happend in 2 batches in the same month?

[assistant.message]
Excellent questions. Here is how it works now.

1. Do you need to wait until the 22nd?
No. You can run invoice on any day.
But the script uses the month + billing_day you pass, not today’s calendar date.

So if you run:
- `op_invoice.sh andres 2026-07 22`
it computes the July billing window anchored on billing day 22 (shifted if weekend), even if today is the 10th.

It will post installments in that window regardless of today’s date.

2. Can you test with assets that started earlier, then run invoice and month-end next day?
Yes, that is a valid and good test.

Recommended test sequence:
1. Enter 3 assets with realistic start dates (some in prior months).
2. Enter current rate row.
3. Run baseline (once).
4. Run daily (any as-of you choose).
5. Run invoice for target month + billing day.
6. Run month-end for same month + billing day.
7. Validate outputs.

How you know it worked:
1. `invoices_YYYY-MM.csv` exists and has rows.
2. `posted_invoices.csv` gained posted rows.
3. `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx` refreshed.
4. `bank_payable.csv` has one row for that month.
5. `closed_periods.csv` includes the month.
6. New manifest files exist.

3. If you invoice “early” (example day 10), what happens to due dates after billing cutoff?
Key point: what matters is the billing_day parameter, not the day you execute command.

If billing_day is 22:
1. Invoice run includes due dates in that billing window up to the billing date.
2. Due dates after that window roll into the next billing cycle.

Can invoices happen in 2 batches in same month?
Technically yes, but operationally you should avoid it.
Why:
1. Script upserts by asset+due date, so reruns can replace rows, not cleanly represent separate batch events.
2. Training/operations model is one invoice-day batch per cycle.

Best practice:
1. One invoice run per billing cycle.
2. Then one month-end run.
3. Do not split into multiple invoice batches unless you intentionally run an exception workflow.

If you want, I can give you a concrete “test case with expected due dates in/out of July billing window” so Andres and Ana can see exactly what should post and what should defer.

[user.message]
get it. So, every daily run, will keep track of each asset's due date and warn if an asset is due, and save that info for the invoicing batch?

[assistant.message]
Almost. Small correction:

1. Yes, daily run tracks and reports upcoming billing context and due workload.
2. No, daily run does not save or post due items into ledger history.
3. Invoice-day run is the step that actually posts and saves invoice history.

So:
- `daily` = monitor/remind
- `invoice` = persist posted rows
- `month-end` = payable + close control

Daily helps you not miss due items, but ledger state changes only on invoice-day/month-end runs.

[user.message]
what does daily run do then?

[assistant.message]
Daily run is your monitoring and decision checkpoint. It does not post accounting history.

What it does:
1. Reads current inputs and current posted history.
2. Recomputes current portfolio state.
3. Prints portfolio totals.
4. Determines the billing month/date context.
5. Shows invoice lines due and amount due for that billing window.
6. Shows reminder if invoice day is tomorrow or today.
7. Writes a daily manifest for traceability.

What it does not do:
1. Does not write invoice rows to posted ledger.
2. Does not close month.
3. Does not update bank payable.
4. Does not replace invoice-day or month-end runs.

In short:
Daily = awareness and control signal.
Invoice-day = posting.
Month-end = payable + lock.

[user.message]
ahhh so billing will update daily (portfolio), but invoices only when creating the batch. So it will update porfolio (inventory tab on the workflow), but will create invoices batch only when its script is run?

[assistant.message]
Exactly right.

1. Daily run updates the live portfolio view and monitoring outputs.
That includes what you’ll see in portfolio metrics and due context.

2. Invoice rows are created and posted only when invoice-day script runs.

3. Month-end then updates bank payable and closes the period.

So yes:
- portfolio monitoring moves daily,
- invoice batch is created only on invoice script execution.

[user.message]
ahhh so billing will update daily (portfolio), but invoices only when creating the batch. So it will update porfolio (inventory tab on the worksheet xls), but will create invoices batch only when its script is run?

[assistant.message]
Exactly right on invoices, and one important nuance on the workbook:

1. Portfolio status is recalculated on every daily run.
2. Invoice files and posted ledger are created/updated only when the invoice-day script runs.
3. Month-end files are updated only when month-end script runs.

Nuance:
1. Daily run recalculates and prints portfolio status, but it does not currently refresh the Excel workbook file.
2. The workbook (Inventory and asset tabs) is automatically refreshed by the invoice-day run.

So:
- daily = recalculation + monitoring output
- invoice-day = posting + workbook refresh
- month-end = bank payable + close lock

[user.message]
but what file does daily run modify? what does it recalculate, which output changes?

[assistant.message]
Great precision. Today, daily run does this:

What it recalculates:
1. Current portfolio snapshot from:
- `assets.csv`
- `rates.csv`
- posted history in `posted_invoices.csv`
2. Billing context for the cycle:
- billing month
- billing date
- lines/amount due in billing window

What file it modifies:
1. It writes a new run manifest JSON in `run_manifests/`.
2. It does not modify business/control/output CSVs.
3. It does not update the Excel workbook file.

So after daily run, changed output is:
1. Terminal report output (for operator decision-making)
2. A new manifest record (audit trace)

Not changed by daily run:
1. `posted_invoices.csv`
2. `closed_periods.csv`
3. `bank_payable.csv`
4. `invoices_YYYY-MM.csv`
5. `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx`

[user.message]
give me an example of the billing context, because it seems there is no need to run daily script if does not modify anything

[assistant.message]
Perfect point. Daily matters because it gives you decision context before posting day, even if it does not write ledger rows.

Example billing context (billing day = 22):
1. Today (`as-of`) = 2026-07-21
2. Daily run prints:
- billing_month: 2026-07
- billing_date: 2026-07-22
- invoice_lines_due: 7
- invoice_amount_due: 18,540.22
- reminder: invoice run due tomorrow

Operational value:
1. You see tomorrow’s batch size and value before posting.
2. You can detect problems early:
- missing asset fields
- unexpected due count
- abnormal amount jump
3. You can fix inputs before invoice-day run, avoiding bad postings.

If you skip daily:
1. You only discover issues when running invoice-day.
2. That can delay billing, force reruns, or create correction work.

So daily is not for posting; it is your pre-flight control check.

Think of it like:
1. Daily = forecast + alert + sanity check
2. Invoice-day = commit
3. Month-end = lock and reconcile

[user.message]
so it does calculate how many assets are due since past invoicing date (billing) to such as-of date, also amount

[assistant.message]
Yes, exactly.

Daily run calculates, for the current billing context:
1. how many installment lines are due in the billing window
2. the total amount due for those lines

It does this from:
1. current assets
2. current rates
3. posted history already in ledger

But it only reports those numbers; it does not post them.

What happens if I include it with that date since the beginning? will be used in second invoicing period?

[assistant.message]
Yes. If you include the second rate row from the beginning, it will be used automatically once its effective date is reached.

Example:
1. `2026-07-06,0.051`
2. `2026-08-01,0.053`

Behavior:
1. July billing cycle uses 0.051 for due periods before Aug 1.
2. From Aug 1 onward, open/future periods use 0.053.
3. Already posted/closed history does not change.
4. Second invoicing period will reflect the new rate where applicable.

So adding it early is fine; it won’t apply too soon, only from its effective date forward.

[user.message]
1. I dont understand, why if ran on 07/09, there were 2 lines since it was due only one asset?

[assistant.message]
Because the daily run is not counting “what is due **today**.” It is counting what belongs to the **current billing batch**.

On `2026-07-09`, the script looked at the July billing window for billing day `22` and found:

1. `A1001` due on `2026-07-10`
2. `A1002` due on `2026-07-21`

Both of those are still inside the July billing batch, because both due dates are before the billing date `2026-07-22`.

So the daily run showed **2 invoice lines due**, even though on `07/09` neither one was actually due *that day* yet.

The rule is:

1. **Daily run** = shows all items that will be invoiced in the current billing window.
2. **Billing date** = the shared cutoff date for that batch.
3. **Asset due date** = each asset’s own anniversary date.

In your example:

- Windblower due `07/10`
- Snow Blower due `07/21`
- Golf Cart due `07/25`

So on `07/09`, the July batch already includes the first two assets, but not the third one.

If you want, I can draw this as a tiny timeline so it becomes visually obvious for Andres and Ana.

[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]
Running daily again on the same date is safe. It is read-only for the business data: it loads `assets`, `rates`, and `posted_invoices`, recomputes the report, prints the same forecast, and writes a new run manifest only. It does not create invoice rows, bank payable rows, or close periods. The daily path is in [alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](alc_item_sheet_tracker.py#L1400), and the report is built from the current posted ledger at [build_snapshot](alc_item_sheet_tracker.py#L700).

So if you rerun `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22` again, you should expect:
1. The same July batch summary if the inputs have not changed.
2. A new manifest file with a new timestamp.
3. No duplicate invoice posting or month closure.

The only thing that changes the downstream state is `op_invoice.sh` or `op_month_end.sh`. Invoice posting is the step that locks history into `posted_invoices.csv` at [run_invoice](alc_item_sheet_tracker.py#L1152), and month-end then closes the period.

If you want, I can also tell you what the rerun would show for each of the three dates specifically, before and after the invoice step.

[user.message]
invoice.csv is the file the agent/operator will send each month to accounting to create separate invoices per property and send by email? whereas the posted invoices is the invoicing log?

[assistant.message]
Yes. That is the right way to think about them, with one important nuance.

[invoices_2026-07.csv](/Users/germanmontoya/Python/vscode/ALC/invoices_2026-07.csv) or the monthly invoice export file is the operational batch output for that billing cycle. It is the file Accounting would use to prepare or issue the actual customer invoices by property. In your current design, it is not an email engine by itself and it does not generate one PDF per property automatically. It is the structured monthly invoice-detail export that supports that process.

[posted_invoices.csv](/Users/germanmontoya/Python/vscode/ALC/posted_invoices.csv) is the internal posted ledger, meaning the invoicing log of what was already invoiced and posted into ALC history. That file is what prevents the system from treating those same installments as still open forecast items, and it is also what lets later portfolio views show balances after posted activity.

So the clean distinction is:

1. [invoices_2026-07.csv](/Users/germanmontoya/Python/vscode/ALC/invoices_2026-07.csv): monthly outbound billing batch for Accounting to act on
2. [posted_invoices.csv](/Users/germanmontoya/Python/vscode/ALC/posted_invoices.csv): internal historical record of invoiced installments already posted

If you want to explain it in class, I would phrase it this way:

- “Invoice file” = what we send out for billing work
- “Posted invoice ledger” = what we keep as system history

One clarification though: if Accounting needs “separate invoices per property and send by email,” the current system stops one step before that. It gives the monthly lines needed for that process, but it does not yet produce formatted per-property invoice documents or send emails automatically.

If you want, I can next help you define whether the monthly invoice export should remain a line-level CSV or become a property-grouped invoice package for Accounting.

we need to update this too then Yes. Here is the class design I would use for that drill.

**Goal**  
Show Andres and Ana how ALC behaves across:
1. daily monitoring before and after due dates
2. invoice posting in 2 billing cycles
3. month-end close and bank payable tracking

**Use these 3 assets**
1. Windblower at `WWE`
2. Snow Blower at `CHA`
3. Golf Cart at `LPA`

**Use billing day 22**
This keeps the billing cycle simple and lets you show carry-forward behavior clearly.

**What the class should prove**
1. Daily run forecasts what is due in the current billing window.
2. Invoice-day run posts only the items that belong in that billing batch.
3. Month-end run updates bank payable and locks the month.
4. A late due date rolls into the next invoice cycle.

---

## Class Flow

**1. Show the clean workspace first**
Have them open only:
- assets.csv
- rates.csv
- scripts
- run_manifests
- backups
- outputs if you keep it as a folder for teaching

What to say:
- inputs are ready
- outputs are empty
- audit folders are empty
- no history yet

---

**2. Use the three assets to create three billing situations**
With your chosen dates:
1. Windblower due around the 10th
2. Snow Blower due around the 21st
3. Golf Cart due around the 25th

That gives you:
1. one asset due before invoice day
2. one asset due right before invoice day
3. one asset due after invoice day, so it rolls to the next cycle

---

**3. Run several daily snapshots**
Use daily runs on simulated as-of dates like these:

1. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22`
2. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-10 22`
3. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22`
4. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22`
5. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-25 22`
6. `op_daily.sh german 2026-08-21 22`

What to show in terminal:
- portfolio totals
- billing month
- billing date
- invoice lines due
- invoice amount due
- reminder when invoice day is tomorrow or today

What to explain:
- daily run is a preview/control step
- it does not post invoice history
- it helps them see what will be invoiced in the next batch

What to watch for:
- on `2026-07-21`, it should warn that invoice day is tomorrow
- on `2026-07-23`, it should already be previewing the next billing cycle
- on `2026-07-25`, the late asset should be part of the next cycle, not the July batch

---

**4. Run the first invoice cycle**
Use:
- `op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22`

What it should do:
- create invoices_2026-07.csv
- update posted_invoices.csv
- refresh ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx
- write manifest files
- create backups if needed

What to tell them:
- this is the commit step
- this is where the month’s billed rows become history

What should post in cycle 1:
- the assets due up to the July billing window cutoff
- the asset due after the cutoff should stay for the next cycle

---

**5. Run month-end for July**
Use:
- `op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22`

What it should do:
- update bank_payable.csv
- add July to closed_periods.csv
- create manifest and backup trace

What to show:
- bank payable total
- one row for the month
- month now locked

---

**6. Run the second invoice cycle**
Use:
- `op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- post the late July due asset plus August cycle items that belong in the billing window
- create invoices_2026-08.csv
- update posted_invoices.csv
- refresh workbook again

This is the key teaching moment:
- the asset due after July billing day is not lost
- it appears in the next invoice cycle

---

**7. Run month-end for August**
Use:
- `op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- add August row to bank_payable.csv
- close August in closed_periods.csv

---

## What to inspect after each stage

**After daily runs**
- terminal shows billing month/date and due amount
- no ledger is posted yet

**After invoice runs**
- posted_invoices.csv grows
- outputs/invoices_YYYY-MM.csv appears
- workbook is refreshed

**After month-end runs**
- bank_payable.csv gets one row per month
- closed_periods.csv gets the closed month

---

## How to explain it in class

Use this story:
1. Daily run tells us what is coming.
2. Invoice-day run commits what belongs to this billing batch.
3. Month-end run locks and reconciles the month.
4. Anything due after the billing cutoff waits for the next invoice cycle.

Use this updated deck text.

**Slide 11: Daily Run**
- Command:
  - `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22`
- Daily run monitors, but does not post accounting history
- Daily run prints:
  - portfolio totals
  - month to invoice
  - billing date
  - billing window due dates
  - invoice lines due
  - invoice amount due
  - reminder if invoice day is tomorrow or today

Presenter note:
Daily run is a preview and control step. It shows what belongs in the current billing window, not only what is due that exact day.

**Slide 12: Invoice-Day Run**
- Command:
  - `./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22`
- Invoice-day run:
  - reads current assets and rates
  - reads posted history first
  - determines the billing date for the month
  - posts invoice rows due in the billing window
  - creates the monthly invoice CSV handoff for a third person to process
  - updates the posted invoice ledger as internal invoicing history
  - refreshes the workbook automatically

Presenter note:
Invoice-day posts history. It does not close the month.

**Slide 13: Month-End Run**
- Command:
  - `./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22`
- Month-end run:
  - updates bank_payable.csv
  - stores one row per month
  - closes the month in closed_periods.csv
  - writes manifests and backups

Presenter note:
Month-end records payable and locks the month. It is the control step.

**Slide 14: Workbook and Outputs**
- Workbook: ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx
- First tab: Inventory
- Remaining tabs: one tab per asset
- Inventory shows the portfolio as of the workbook snapshot date and displays that as-of date
- Asset tabs show lease inputs, lifecycle status, and schedule state
- Invoice-day refreshes the workbook automatically
- invoices_YYYY-MM.csv is the monthly outbound billing file for processing
- posted_invoices.csv is the internal posted invoice history log

Presenter note:
Operators should review the workbook, not maintain it by hand.

**Slide 15: Class Drill Sequence**
- Start from a clean workspace: inputs ready, outputs empty, no history yet
- Use the three training assets: Windblower at WWE, Snow Blower at CHA, Golf Cart at LPA
- Use billing day 22 for all steps
- Run the sequence in order:
  - `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22`
  - `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22`
  - `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22`
  - `./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22`
  - `./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22`
  - `./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22`
  - `./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22`

Presenter note:
This drill proves preview, post, close, and carry-forward behavior across two billing cycles.

**Slide 16: Validation Checklist**
- assets.csv and rates.csv are valid
- invoice CSV exists after invoice-day run
- posted_invoices.csv has new rows after invoice-day run
- workbook refreshed and Inventory tab is first
- bank_payable.csv has one row for the month after month-end
- closed_periods.csv includes the month after month-end
- manifest and backups exist after write operations

Presenter note:
Validation is part of the job, not an optional extra.

**Slide 17: Common Errors**
- Running inside Python >>> instead of shell terminal
- Wrong date format in CSV
- Missing or wrong file path
- Confusing due date with billing date
- Using `.op_...` instead of `./scripts/op_...`
- Trying to post a closed month
- Trying to invoice before go-live
- Expecting weekend billing to stay on a weekend date

If you want, I can also give you a shorter speaker version with exactly what to say aloud for each of those slides.

[assistant.message]
Use this updated full class-design text.

Yes. Here is the class design I would use for that drill.

**Goal**  
Show Andres and Ana how ALC behaves across:
1. daily monitoring before and after due dates
2. invoice posting in 2 billing cycles
3. month-end close and bank payable tracking

**Use these 3 assets**
1. Windblower at `WWE`
2. Snow Blower at `CHA`
3. Golf Cart at `LPA`

**Use billing day 22**  
This keeps the billing cycle simple and lets you show carry-forward behavior clearly.

**What the class should prove**
1. Daily run forecasts what is due in the current billing window.
2. Invoice-day run posts only the items that belong in that billing batch.
3. Month-end run updates bank payable and locks the month.
4. A late due date rolls into the next invoice cycle.

---

## Class Flow

**1. Show the clean workspace first**  
Have them open only:
- `assets.csv`
- `rates.csv`
- `scripts/`
- `run_manifests/`
- `backups/`

What to say:
- inputs are ready
- outputs are empty
- audit folders are empty
- no history yet

---

**2. Use the three assets to create three billing situations**  
With your chosen dates:
1. Windblower due around the 10th
2. Snow Blower due around the 21st
3. Golf Cart due around the 25th

That gives you:
1. one asset due before invoice day
2. one asset due right before invoice day
3. one asset due after invoice day, so it rolls to the next cycle

---

**3. Run several daily snapshots**  
Use daily runs on simulated as-of dates like these:

1. `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22`
2. `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22`
3. `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22`

If you want the extended version for class discussion, you can also show:
4. `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-25 22`
5. `./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-08-21 22`

What to show in terminal:
- portfolio totals
- month to invoice
- billing date
- billing window due dates
- invoice lines due
- invoice amount due
- reminder when invoice day is tomorrow or today

What to explain:
- daily run is a preview/control step
- it does not post invoice history
- it helps them see what will be invoiced in the next batch
- the due count is based on the billing window, not only on what is due that exact day

What to watch for:
- on `2026-07-21`, it should warn that invoice day is tomorrow
- on `2026-07-23`, it should already be previewing the next billing cycle
- on `2026-07-25`, the late asset should be part of the next cycle, not the July batch

---

**4. Run the first invoice cycle**  
Use:
- `./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22`

What it should do:
- create `invoices_2026-07.csv`
- update `posted_invoices.csv`
- refresh `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx`
- write manifest files
- create backups if needed

What to tell them:
- this is the commit step
- this is where the month’s billed rows become history
- `invoices_2026-07.csv` is the monthly handoff file for a third person to process
- `posted_invoices.csv` is the internal invoicing log

What should post in cycle 1:
- the assets due up to the July billing window cutoff
- the asset due after the cutoff should stay for the next cycle

What to inspect:
- `invoices_2026-07.csv`
- `posted_invoices.csv`
- `ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx`

Important workbook note:
- the Inventory tab should show the portfolio as of the billing-date snapshot
- the sheet should display that as-of date
- balances should no longer be full original balances after posting

---

**5. Run month-end for July**  
Use:
- `./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22`

What it should do:
- update `bank_payable.csv`
- add July to `closed_periods.csv`
- create manifest and backup trace

What to show:
- bank payable total
- one row for the month
- month now locked

Important clarification:
- this wrapper runs two internal actions:
  1. bank payable
  2. close period
- that is why one month-end wrapper creates two manifests

---

**6. Run the second invoice cycle**  
Use:
- `./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- post the late July due asset plus August cycle items that belong in the billing window
- create `invoices_2026-08.csv`
- update `posted_invoices.csv`
- refresh workbook again

This is the key teaching moment:
- the asset due after July billing day is not lost
- it appears in the next invoice cycle

What to tell them:
- again, `invoices_2026-08.csv` is the outbound monthly processing file
- `posted_invoices.csv` is the internal posted history

---

**7. Run month-end for August**  
Use:
- `./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- add August row to `bank_payable.csv`
- close August in `closed_periods.csv`

---

## What to inspect after each stage

**After daily runs**
- terminal shows month to invoice, billing date, billing window due dates, and due amount
- no ledger is posted yet
- only a manifest is created

**After invoice runs**
- `posted_invoices.csv` grows
- `invoices_YYYY-MM.csv` appears
- workbook is refreshed
- Inventory reflects the billing-date snapshot

**After month-end runs**
- `bank_payable.csv` gets one row per month
- `closed_periods.csv` gets the closed month

---

## How to explain it in class

Use this story:
1. Daily run tells us what is coming in the current billing window.
2. Invoice-day run commits what belongs to this billing batch.
3. Month-end run locks and reconciles the month.
4. Anything due after the billing cutoff waits for the next invoice cycle.

---

## One-line summary for Andres and Ana
- Daily = preview
- Invoice day = post
- Month-end = close
- Later due dates roll into the next cycle

If you want, I can also convert this into a tighter presenter script with short spoken lines under each step.

[user.message]
no code, just question: where can I validate interest paid total up to that run?

[assistant.message]
Great question. You can validate it in 2 levels:

1. Exact value used by that run:
- Open the manifest JSON created by that daily command in [run_manifests](run_manifests).
- Look under portfolio_totals.interest_paid_total.
- That is the authoritative snapshot value for that run timestamp.

2. Supporting detail behind the total:
- Asset-by-asset posted history: [posted_invoices.csv](posted_invoices.csv) and sum interest by rows up to the run context.
- Forward-looking per-asset schedule view (for training explanation): the asset tabs in [ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx](ALC%20-%20Asset%20Calculation%20Unit.xlsx).

Important nuance:
- For a daily preview run, interest_paid_total is the portfolio cumulative amount as of the as-of date, not only “already invoiced rows.”  
- So for audit trace of that exact displayed number, the manifest is the first place to confirm.


If you want to run the full 7-step class sequence with separators, use:

```bash
for cmd in \
  "./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22" \
  "./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22" \
  "./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22" \
  "./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22" \
  "./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22" \
  "./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22" \
  "./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22"
do
  echo ""
  echo "______________"
  echo ""
  echo "$cmd"
  eval "$cmd"
done
```

# ALC Operations Training

## Slide 1: Title
- ALC Operations Training
- Running ALC safely from clean go-live through daily monitoring, invoice-day posting, and month-end close
- Internal training for Ana and Andres

Presenter note:
Set the tone: this is a no-code operator workflow. The goal is to use only the approved files and wrapper scripts.

## Slide 2: Class Goal
- Teach operators how ALC works from a zero-record baseline
- Show which files operators edit and which files the system controls
- Practice the 3-step recurring operating cycle
- Build confidence in validation and auditability

Presenter note:
Stress that production starts clean. History begins only when real operations begin.

## Slide 3: Business Story
- We receive or update assets and bank rates
- Each asset begins a lease life based on its activation date
- The system calculates expected installments across that life
- Daily we monitor the portfolio and upcoming billing activity
- On billing day we post invoices in one batch
- At month-end we record bank payable and close the month
- The system preserves history, locks closed months, and leaves an audit trail

Presenter note:
Repeat the message: users manage business inputs, the system manages controls and outputs.

## Slide 4: The 3-Step Operating Cycle
- Every day: run the daily script
- On invoice day: run the invoice-day script
- At month-end: run the month-end script

Presenter note:
Keep this simple. Routine operations use only three recurring commands after baseline.

## Slide 5: Due Date vs Billing Date vs Month Close
- Due date: each asset has its own anniversary-based installment date
- Billing date: one monthly batch invoicing date, default example 22nd
- Weekend rule: billing date shifts to the next working day
- Month close: a separate control step that locks the month

Presenter note:
This is the most important concept to avoid confusion. Asset due date is not the same as billing date.

## Slide 6: Learning Objectives
- Identify the core files and their purpose
- Explain due date, billing date, and month close
- Run baseline once before production
- Run daily, invoice-day, and month-end wrappers
- Validate outputs and audit traces
- Recognize common operating mistakes

Presenter note:
Make it clear they do not need to use developer commands or non-routine helper scripts.

## Slide 7: File Map
### User-managed inputs
- assets.csv
- rates.csv

### System and control files
- baseline_config.json
- posted_invoices.csv
- closed_periods.csv

### Operational outputs
- invoices_YYYY-MM.csv
- bank_payable.csv
- ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx

### Audit and traceability
- run_manifests/
- backups/

Presenter note:
Only assets and rates are operator-maintained business inputs.

## Slide 8: What Operators Edit vs Never Edit
### Operators edit
- assets.csv
- rates.csv

### Operators do not edit manually
- posted_invoices.csv
- closed_periods.csv
- bank_payable.csv
- files in run_manifests/
- files in backups/

Presenter note:
This slide is a guardrail. If they remember only one file rule, it should be this one.

## Slide 9: Clean Baseline and Go-Live
- Baseline is a one-time clean production start
- Posted invoice history starts empty
- Closed periods start empty
- Bank payable history starts empty
- No fake or sample history should remain in live production files
- Real history begins only after go-live activity

Presenter note:
Baseline is not an invoice run and not a month close.

## Slide 10: Baseline Command
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_init_baseline.sh german 2026-07-01 "Production start"
- Expected result:
  - baseline config created
  - posted ledger reset to header only
  - closed periods reset to header only
  - manifest created

Presenter note:
This is a one-time operation before production use.

## Slide 11: Daily Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22
- Daily run monitors, but does not post accounting history
- Wrapper output includes visual separators for readability:
  - blank space before and after each run
  - line separator: ______________
- Daily run prints:
  - portfolio totals
  - month to invoice
  - billing date
  - billing window due dates
  - invoice lines due
  - invoice amount due
  - reminder if invoice day is tomorrow or today

Presenter note:
Daily run keeps the process from depending on memory.

## Slide 12: Invoice-Day Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22
- Invoice-day run:
  - reads current assets and rates
  - reads posted history first
  - determines the billing date for the month
  - posts invoice rows due in the billing window
  - creates the monthly invoice CSV handoff for a third person to process
  - updates the posted invoice ledger as internal invoicing history
  - refreshes the workbook automatically

Presenter note:
Invoice-day posts history. It does not close the month.

## Slide 13: Month-End Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22
- Month-end run:
  - updates bank_payable.csv
  - stores one row per month
  - closes the month in closed_periods.csv
  - writes manifests and backups

Presenter note:
Month-end records payable and locks the month. It is the control step.

## Slide 14: Workbook and Outputs
- Workbook: ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx
- First tab: Inventory
- Remaining tabs: one tab per asset
- Inventory shows the portfolio as of the workbook snapshot date and displays that as-of date
- Asset tabs show lease inputs, lifecycle status, and schedule state
- Invoice-day refreshes the workbook automatically
- invoices_YYYY-MM.csv is the monthly outbound billing file for processing
- posted_invoices.csv is the internal posted invoice history log

Presenter note:
Operators should review the workbook, not maintain it by hand.

## Slide 15: Class Drill Sequence
- Start from a clean workspace: inputs ready, outputs empty, no history yet
- Use the three training assets: Windblower at WWE, Snow Blower at CHA, Golf Cart at LPA
- Use billing day 22 for all steps
- No manual echo blocks are needed in class; wrappers already print separators
- Run the sequence in order:
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22

Presenter note:
This drill proves preview, post, close, and carry-forward behavior across two billing cycles.

## Slide 16: Validation Checklist
- assets.csv and rates.csv are valid
- invoice CSV exists after invoice-day run
- posted_invoices.csv has new rows after invoice-day run
- workbook refreshed and Inventory tab is first
- bank_payable.csv has one row for the month after month-end
- closed_periods.csv includes the month after month-end
- manifest and backups exist after write operations
- each run is clearly delimited in terminal by separator blocks

Presenter note:
Validation is part of the job, not an optional extra.

## Slide 17: Common Errors
- Running inside Python prompt instead of shell terminal
- Wrong date format in CSV
- Missing or wrong file path
- Confusing due date with billing date
- Using .op_... instead of ./scripts/op_...
- Trying to post a closed month
- Trying to invoice before go-live
- Expecting weekend billing to stay on a weekend date

Presenter note:
Explain what each error means and what the safe next action should be.

## Slide 18: Roles and Guardrails
### Operator
- update assets.csv
- update rates.csv
- run wrappers
- validate outputs

### Admin and owner
- manage baseline and overrides
- handle exceptional corrections
- manage structure changes

### Guardrails
- never edit posted ledger manually in normal operations
- never edit closed periods manually in normal operations
- never create fake production history after baseline

Presenter note:
The workflow should stay boring and repeatable.

## Slide 19: Success Looks Like
- User can explain the 3-step operating cycle
- User can explain due date vs billing date vs month close
- User can identify what they edit and what the system controls
- User can run the routine wrappers without developer help
- User can validate outputs and locate audit evidence

Presenter note:
End by reinforcing simplicity and confidence: same files, same scripts, same checks every cycle.

**ALC Class Rehearsal Script**

Goal  
Teach Andres and Ana the full operating rhythm:
1. Daily run previews the billing window.
2. Invoice run posts the billing batch.
3. Month-end run records payable and closes the month.
4. Items after cutoff roll into next cycle.

Training setup  
1. Use three assets:
- Windblower (WWE)
- Snow Blower (CHA)
- Golf Cart (LPA)

2. Use billing day 22.

3. Start from clean outputs.

What this class must prove  
1. Daily is preview only.
2. Invoice is commit/posting.
3. Month-end is control/close.
4. Cutoff behavior works across two cycles.

------------------------------------------------------------

Class flow (7 runs)

Run 1  
Command: ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22  
Explain:
- It previews July billing window.
- It can show more than one line due in the batch, even before exact due day.

Run 2  
Command: ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22  
Explain:
- Same July window.
- Should show reminder that invoice day is tomorrow.

Run 3  
Command: ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22  
Explain:
- This is the posting step.
- Creates monthly outbound invoice file.
- Updates internal posted ledger.
- Refreshes workbook snapshot.

Run 4  
Command: ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22  
Explain:
- It should now preview August cycle.
- Billing date should move to 2026-08-24.
- Late July due item is now visible in next cycle window.

Run 5  
Command: ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22  
Explain:
- Writes July bank payable row.
- Closes July period.
- One month-end wrapper runs two internal actions, so it creates two manifests.

Run 6  
Command: ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22  
Explain:
- Posts the August batch.
- Includes carry-forward logic from prior cutoff.
- Creates August outbound invoice file.
- Updates posted ledger again.

Run 7  
Command: ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22  
Explain:
- Writes August bank payable row.
- Closes August period.

------------------------------------------------------------

What to verify after each type of run

After daily runs  
1. month_to_invoice
2. billing_date
3. billing_window_due_dates
4. invoice_lines_due
5. invoice_amount_due
6. no posting files changed by daily, only manifest

After invoice runs  
1. invoices_YYYY-MM.csv exists (outbound monthly handoff file)
2. posted_invoices.csv grows (internal invoicing log)
3. workbook refreshes and Inventory reflects billing-date snapshot
4. manifest exists

After month-end runs  
1. bank_payable.csv has one row per month
2. closed_periods.csv includes closed month
3. manifests and backups exist

------------------------------------------------------------

How to explain it in one story  
1. Daily tells us what is coming in the billing window.  
2. Invoice-day commits that batch into history.  
3. Month-end reconciles and locks the month.  
4. Anything after cutoff waits for next cycle.

One-line summary  
Daily = preview  
Invoice day = post  
Month-end = close  
Later due dates roll forward to next cycle

why 7 manifests? 3 x daily runs, 1 x invoicing for the 4 runs

[assistant.message]
I’ll quickly inspect the manifest files themselves so I can show exactly which commands created each one and why the count is higher than 4.

[assistant.message]
Because there were 7 actual command executions that write manifests, not 4 unique steps.

From [run_manifests](run_manifests), these were created:

1. `daily` for `2026-07-09`
2. `daily` for `2026-07-21`
3. `invoice` for `2026-07`
4. `daily` for `2026-07-09` again
5. `invoice` for `2026-07` again
6. `daily` for `2026-07-23`
7. `daily` for `2026-07-23` again

So the extra 3 are duplicates from reruns/smoke checks while we were adjusting wrapper output formatting.

Rule of thumb:
- each [scripts/op_daily.sh](scripts/op_daily.sh) run = 1 manifest
- each [scripts/op_invoice.sh](scripts/op_invoice.sh) run = 1 manifest
- each [scripts/op_month_end.sh](scripts/op_month_end.sh) run = 2 manifests (bank-payable + close-period)

If you want a clean class-only trace, clear [run_manifests](run_manifests) once and continue from the next class step.

- assets.csv
- rates.csv

With your chosen dates:
1. Windblower due around the 10th
2. Snow Blower due around the 21st
3. Golf Cart due around the 25th

That gives you:
1. one asset due before invoice day
2. one asset due right before invoice day
3. one asset due after invoice day, so it rolls to the next cycle

---

**3. Run several daily snapshots**
Use daily runs on simulated as-of dates like these:

1. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22`
2. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-10 22`
3. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22`
4. `op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22`

- portfolio totals
- billing month

- invoice lines due
- invoice amount due
- reminder when invoice day is tomorrow or today

- it does not post invoice history

- write manifest files
- create backups if needed

What to tell them:
- this is the commit step
- this is where the month’s billed rows become history

What should post in cycle 1:
- the assets due up to the July billing window cutoff
- the asset due after the cutoff should stay for the next cycle

---

- `op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22`

What it should do:
- update bank_payable.csv
- add July to closed_periods.csv
- create manifest and backup trace

What to show:
- bank payable total
- one row for the month
- month now locked

---

**6. Run the second invoice cycle**
Use:
- `op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- post the late July due asset plus August cycle items that belong in the billing window
- create invoices_2026-08.csv
- update posted_invoices.csv
- refresh workbook again

This is the key teaching moment:
- the asset due after July billing day is not lost
- it appears in the next invoice cycle

---

**7. Run month-end for August**
Use:
- `op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22`

What it should do:
- add August row to bank_payable.csv
- close August in closed_periods.csv

[user.message]
when was the asset calculation unit created? in the only invoice wrapper?

[assistant.message]
I’ll verify the exact code path now so I can answer precisely whether the workbook is created only by the invoice wrapper or also by other commands.

[assistant.message]
Short answer: not only in invoice wrapper, but invoice wrapper is the usual one in class flow.

How it works:
1. [scripts/op_invoice.sh](scripts/op_invoice.sh) calls the invoice command, and that command always calls workbook generation in [alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](alc_item_sheet_tracker.py#L1262).  
2. [scripts/op_one_pager.sh](scripts/op_one_pager.sh) also calls workbook generation in [alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](alc_item_sheet_tracker.py#L1546).  
3. Daily and month-end wrappers do not create the workbook.

So:
- If [ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx](ALC%20-%20Asset%20Calculation%20Unit.xlsx) does not exist, the first invoice run will create it.
- After that, each invoice run refreshes it.
- You can also create/refresh it manually via the one-pager wrapper.

[user.message]
question, when asset calculation unit was created or updated... how does the inventory tab knows which date was it updated at?

[assistant.message]
Great question. It knows from two places:

1. Inside the workbook itself  
- The Inventory tab has an “As of” line in cell A2.  
- That value is written by the script when the workbook is generated/refreshed.

2. File system timestamp  
- Finder “Date Modified” on [ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx](ALC%20-%20Asset%20Calculation%20Unit.xlsx) tells you when the file was last saved.

How that “As of” date is chosen:
1. If you run [scripts/op_invoice.sh](scripts/op_invoice.sh), Inventory uses the invoice billing date for that cycle.
2. If you run [scripts/op_one_pager.sh](scripts/op_one_pager.sh), it uses today’s date (unless code is changed to pass a specific date).
3. [scripts/op_daily.sh](scripts/op_daily.sh) and [scripts/op_month_end.sh](scripts/op_month_end.sh) do not refresh the workbook.

So for your class flow, the Inventory date should match the latest invoice-cycle billing date after each invoice run.

[user.message]
remind me why we need again those pages (reference) with the same wrappers?

[assistant.message]
You do not need them for “new commands.” You need them for teaching reinforcement.

Why keep those Reference pages:
1. Different purpose, same wrapper:
- one slide explains when to run it
- another explains what output to verify
- another explains common mistakes for that same command

2. Class behavior:
- operators forget sequence under pressure
- repeated wrapper in context helps them map command to outcome fast

3. Role split:
- class flow slides = story and sequence
- reference slides = job aid they can use later without you

If you want leaner deck:
1. Keep one “Reference” divider only once
2. Keep one slide per wrapper:
- Daily: command + expected terminal fields
- Invoice: command + output files
- Month-end: command + lock controls
3. Move the rest (validation/errors) into one final checklist slide

So repetition is optional. It is useful for training retention, but you can simplify it if your audience is already comfortable.

[user.message]
ok, storytelling suggests to run all 3 daily runs before the invoicind despite invoicing is 22nd and last daily dates 23th. Is there any change if run according to schedule?

[assistant.message]
Yes, there is a real change in meaning if you run `07-23` daily before the `07` invoice.

If you follow real schedule:
1. `07-21` daily is July preview (`month_to_invoice = 2026-07-01`)
2. `07-22` invoice posts July
3. `07-23` daily becomes August preview (`month_to_invoice = 2026-08-01`)

If you run all 3 dailies first (including `07-23`) before July invoice:
1. `07-23` daily still previews August
2. but July has not been posted yet in that storyline
3. this can confuse trainees because the narrative jumps ahead before showing the July commit step

So for teaching, best sequence is:
1. daily `07-09`
2. daily `07-21`
3. invoice `2026-07`
4. daily `07-23`
5. month-end `2026-07`
6. invoice `2026-08`
7. month-end `2026-08`

That keeps chronology and control logic clean.
