[assistant.message]
Here is the regenerated class deck text, fully updated.

# ALC Operations Training

## Slide 1: Title
- ALC Operations Training
- Running ALC safely from clean go-live through daily monitoring, invoice-day posting, and month-end close
- Internal training for Ana and Andres

Presenter note:
Set the tone: this is a no-code operator workflow. The goal is to use only the approved files and wrapper scripts.

## Slide 2: Class Goal
- Teach operators how ALC works from a zero-record baseline
- Show which files operators edit and which files the system controls
- Practice the 3-step recurring operating cycle
- Build confidence in validation and auditability

Presenter note:
Stress that production starts clean. History begins only when real operations begin.

## Slide 3: Business Story
- We receive or update assets and bank rates
- Each asset begins a lease life based on its activation date
- The system calculates expected installments across that life
- Daily we monitor the portfolio and upcoming billing activity
- On billing day we post invoices in one batch
- At month-end we record bank payable and close the month
- The system preserves history, locks closed months, and leaves an audit trail

Presenter note:
Repeat the message: users manage business inputs, the system manages controls and outputs.

## Slide 4: The 3-Step Operating Cycle
- Every day: run the daily script
- On invoice day: run the invoice-day script
- At month-end: run the month-end script

Presenter note:
Keep this simple. Routine operations use only three recurring commands after baseline.

## Slide 5: Due Date vs Billing Date vs Month Close
- Due date: each asset has its own anniversary-based installment date
- Billing date: one monthly batch invoicing date, default example 22nd
- Weekend rule: billing date shifts to the next working day
- Month close: a separate control step that locks the month

Presenter note:
This is the most important concept to avoid confusion. Asset due date is not the same as billing date.

## Slide 6: Learning Objectives
- Identify the core files and their purpose
- Explain due date, billing date, and month close
- Run baseline once before production
- Run daily, invoice-day, and month-end wrappers
- Validate outputs and audit traces
- Recognize common operating mistakes

Presenter note:
Make it clear they do not need to use developer commands or non-routine helper scripts.

## Slide 7: File Map
### User-managed inputs
- assets.csv
- rates.csv

### System and control files
- baseline_config.json
- posted_invoices.csv
- closed_periods.csv

### Operational outputs
- invoices_YYYY-MM.csv
- bank_payable.csv
- ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx

### Audit and traceability
- run_manifests/
- backups/

Presenter note:
Only assets and rates are operator-maintained business inputs.

## Slide 8: What Operators Edit vs Never Edit
### Operators edit
- assets.csv
- rates.csv

### Operators do not edit manually
- posted_invoices.csv
- closed_periods.csv
- bank_payable.csv
- files in run_manifests/
- files in backups/

Presenter note:
This slide is a guardrail. If they remember only one file rule, it should be this one.

## Slide 9: Clean Baseline and Go-Live
- Baseline is a one-time clean production start
- Posted invoice history starts empty
- Closed periods start empty
- Bank payable history starts empty
- No fake or sample history should remain in live production files
- Real history begins only after go-live activity

Presenter note:
Baseline is not an invoice run and not a month close.

## Slide 10: Baseline Command
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_init_baseline.sh german 2026-07-01 "Production start"
- Expected result:
  - baseline config created
  - posted ledger reset to header only
  - closed periods reset to header only
  - manifest created

Presenter note:
This is a one-time operation before production use.

## Slide 11: Daily Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22
- Daily run monitors, but does not post accounting history
- Wrapper output includes visual separators for readability:
  - blank space before and after each run
  - line separator: ______________
- Daily run prints:
  - portfolio totals
  - month to invoice
  - billing date
  - billing window due dates
  - invoice lines due
  - invoice amount due
  - reminder if invoice day is tomorrow or today

Presenter note:
Daily run keeps the process from depending on memory.

## Slide 12: Invoice-Day Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22
- Invoice-day run:
  - reads current assets and rates
  - reads posted history first
  - determines the billing date for the month
  - posts invoice rows due in the billing window
  - creates the monthly invoice CSV handoff for a third person to process
  - updates the posted invoice ledger as internal invoicing history
  - refreshes the workbook automatically

Presenter note:
Invoice-day posts history. It does not close the month.

## Slide 13: Month-End Run
- Command:
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22
- Month-end run:
  - updates bank_payable.csv
  - stores one row per month
  - closes the month in closed_periods.csv
  - writes two manifests (bank-payable and close-period) and backups

Presenter note:
Month-end records payable and locks the month. It is the control step.

## Slide 14: Workbook and Outputs
- Workbook: ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx
- First tab: Inventory
- Remaining tabs: one tab per asset
- Inventory shows the portfolio as of the workbook snapshot date and displays that as-of date
- Asset tabs show lease inputs, lifecycle status, and schedule state
- Invoice-day refreshes the workbook automatically
- invoices_YYYY-MM.csv is the monthly outbound billing file for processing
- posted_invoices.csv is the internal posted invoice history log

Presenter note:
Operators should review the workbook, not maintain it by hand.

## Slide 15: Class Drill Sequence
- Start from a clean workspace: inputs ready, outputs empty, no history yet
- Use the three training assets: Windblower at WWE, Snow Blower at CHA, Golf Cart at LPA
- Use billing day 22 for all steps
- No manual echo blocks are needed in class; wrappers already print separators
- Run the sequence in order:
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-09 22
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-21 22
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-07 22
  - ./scripts/op_daily.sh german 2026-07-23 22
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-07 22
  - ./scripts/op_invoice.sh german 2026-08 22
  - ./scripts/op_month_end.sh german 2026-08 22

Presenter note:
This drill proves preview, post, close, and carry-forward behavior across two billing cycles.

## Slide 16: Validation Checklist
- assets.csv and rates.csv are valid
- invoice CSV exists after invoice-day run
- posted_invoices.csv has new rows after invoice-day run
- workbook refreshed and Inventory tab is first
- bank_payable.csv has one row for the month after month-end
- closed_periods.csv includes the month after month-end
- manifest and backups exist after write operations
- month-end run adds two manifests (bank-payable and close-period)
- each run is clearly delimited in terminal by separator blocks

Presenter note:
Validation is part of the job, not an optional extra.

## Slide 17: Common Errors
- Running inside Python >>> instead of shell terminal
- Wrong date format in CSV
- Missing or wrong file path
- Confusing due date with billing date
- Using .op_... instead of ./scripts/op_...
- Trying to post a closed month
- Trying to invoice before go-live
- Expecting weekend billing to stay on a weekend date

Presenter note:
Explain what each error means and what the safe next action should be.

## Slide 18: Roles and Guardrails
### Operator
- update assets.csv
- update rates.csv
- run wrappers
- validate outputs

### Admin and owner
- manage baseline and overrides
- handle exceptional corrections
- manage structure changes

### Guardrails
- never edit posted ledger manually in normal operations
- never edit closed periods manually in normal operations
- never create fake production history after baseline

Presenter note:
The workflow should stay boring and repeatable.

## Slide 19: Success Looks Like
- User can explain the 3-step operating cycle
- User can explain due date vs billing date vs month close
- User can identify what they edit and what the system controls
- User can run the routine wrappers without developer help
- User can validate outputs and locate audit evidence

Presenter note:
End by reinforcing simplicity and confidence: same files, same scripts, same checks every cycle.

Perfect, let’s explain each point against the current script behavior before changing anything.

1. Why 25 lifespan if expected 24
- In the script, lifespan shown on asset tabs comes from term_months (raw contract term).
- The actual invoicing count uses payment_months = term_months - salvage_periods.
- So if term_months is 25 and salvage_periods is 1, invoiced periods become 24.
- You are correct conceptually: term to maturity should track remaining invoiced periods, not raw term.
- Right now:
  - Inventory shows Term Months (raw term).
  - Schedule rows use term_to_maturity (invoicing horizon).
- If salvage is not a real business requirement, we should simplify and remove it. Otherwise, keep it but label both values clearly.

2. As-of date only in Inventory tab
- You are correct. Current behavior writes As of only on Inventory.
- Asset tabs do not currently show:
  - As-of date
  - visual split between posted (invoiced) and projected (future) rows
- This is a valid gap for class clarity and should be added.

3. Asset 3 “starts in June, not July”
- Current logic treats start_date as contract start date.
- Asset tab includes an opening row on start_date (period 0), then first due installment at start_date + 1 month.
- So a June start naturally gives first due in July.
- If business wants “start date” to mean “first installment date”, then logic must change.
- Today’s logic is consistent with contract-start model, not first-installment model.

4. Start date alignment, valid invoicing periods, rate selection
- Start date on asset tabs currently comes from assets.csv and is used as opening row date.
- Invoicing uses only due dates inside billing window, so invalid early periods are not posted.
- Rate logic:
  - Open periods: latest effective rate <= that period month.
  - Posted periods: reuse posted row values (locked history).
- Why June appears for July-active assets:
  - Because contract start is June, while first installment due is July.

5. Inventory should show both term and term to maturity
- Correct. Script already calculates months_remaining (remaining invoiced periods) in snapshot.
- Inventory currently does not display months_remaining column.
- This is a straightforward enhancement and improves operational tracking significantly.

6. Inactive status at maturity
- Current status is computed dynamically in snapshot:
  - active only if source status is active AND balance > 0 AND as_of >= start_date
- So once balance reaches zero, snapshot status becomes inactive.
- Important nuance:
  - This is not persisted back to assets.csv.
  - It is calculated view-time status, not a stored status update.
- If you need permanent status change in assets.csv after maturity, that is a separate design decision.

Recommendation before coding
1. Confirm business definition:
- Is start_date contract start or first installment date?
2. Confirm simplification choice:
- Keep salvage variables or remove them from model.
3. Confirm output changes:
- Add As-of + posted/future split line on every asset tab.
- Add Term to Maturity column on Inventory.
- Decide whether inactive should remain computed-only or also be written to assets.csv.

If you want, next I can propose an exact “change spec” in 10-12 bullets for your sign-off before touching code.

[assistant.message]
I'll map all the related lifespan/term concepts and trace how each flows through downstream calculations.

**Current lifespan-related terms in the system:**

1. `term_months` (from assets.csv)
   - Raw contract term in months
   - User input
   - Example: 24, 36, 48

2. `salvage_periods` (from assets.csv, optional, defaults to 0)
   - Non-payment periods at end of lease
   - User input
   - Example: 1 (means skip last period for payment)

3. `payment_months` (calculated property)
   - Formula: `term_months - salvage_periods`
   - Used for: invoice count, loop bounds, payment schedule generation
   - Example: 24 - 1 = 23 payment periods

4. `final_month` (calculated property)
   - Formula: `start_date + payment_months` (adds payment_months as months to start_date)
   - Used for: determining asset maturity date, lifecycle status
   - Example: 2026-06-10 + 23 months = 2028-05-10

5. `term_to_maturity` (per-row in schedule)
   - Formula: `payment_months - period_number`
   - Used for: schedule rows, workbook display, posted ledger tracking
   - Example: at period 1, TTM = 23; at period 5, TTM = 19

6. `months_remaining` (in snapshot)
   - Formula: `payment_months - installments_paid`
   - Used for: workbook Inventory tab, tracking progress to maturity
   - Example: after 5 payments, 18 months remain

**Downstream usage map:**

```
term_months + salvage_periods (inputs)
    ↓
payment_months (= term_months - salvage_periods)
    ├→ Schedule generation loop (for i in range(payment_months))
    ├→ final_month calculation (start_date + payment_months)
    ├→ Opening row term_to_maturity value
    └→ Lifecycle status (active only if balance > 0 and as_of >= start_date)
        ↓
    final_month
        └→ Determines asset maturity and inactive status
    ├→ Invoice eligibility check (asset.status == "active")
    └→ Workbook display (Final Date column)
    
For each schedule row (period 1 to payment_months):
    term_to_maturity = payment_months - period_number
        ├→ Posted ledger (term_to_maturity column)
        ├→ Workbook asset tabs (Term to Maturity column)
        └→ Invoice CSV output (term_to_maturity field)

After posting:
    months_remaining = payment_months - installments_paid
        ├→ Workbook Inventory (Term to Maturity column)
        ├→ Snapshot report
        └→ Daily monitoring output
```

**Current problem with this model:**

The issue you raised: if `term_months = 25` and `salvage_periods = 1`, then:
- `payment_months = 24` (correct for invoicing count)
- But workbook displays "Lifespan (months): 25" which looks wrong if you only invoice 24 times

The question is: should "lifespan" mean the raw contract term (25) or the actual invoice count (24)?

**My recommendation for Point 1 decision:**

Option A (current):
- Keep both `term_months` and `salvage_periods` in assets.csv.
- Relabel workbook display: "Contract Term (months): 25" and "Invoiced Periods: 24".
- Pro: full flexibility for salvage models. Con: adds conceptual overhead.

Option B (simplify):
- Remove `salvage_periods` entirely from the model.
- Keep only `term_months`, which now means "invoiced periods" exclusively.
- Update assets.csv to remove the salvage_periods column.
- Pro: simpler mental model. Con: loses ability to model salvage scenarios.

Option C (middle ground):
- Keep `salvage_periods` but hide it from operator view.
- Default to 0 (no salvage).
- Only show "Term Months" and "Term to Maturity" in workbook/docs.
- Pro: retains flexibility for future edge cases. Con: invisible logic.

Which direction aligns with Acento's business model?

[assistant.message]
Excellent clarity. Let me assess the current model against this understanding:

**Your insight: Lifespan - Salvage Periods = Payment Months**

This is correct and well-framed. Assessing the current system:

**What we have (correct):**
- `term_months` = Asset's real useful lifespan (24, 36, 48 months)
- `salvage_periods` = Count of periods at end with no invoice (typically 0 or 1)
- `payment_months = term_months - salvage_periods` = Actual invoicing count
- `start_date` = Anchor for payment due dates (6/25 start → 7/25, 8/25, ... first payments)
- Salvage VALUE ($ amount) already affects Lease Base correctly

**What's unnecessary (the overhead):**
- No separate "contract time" concept needed
- No intermediate derived terms beyond `payment_months` and `final_month`
- `final_month = start_date + payment_months` is purely for display/status logic; doesn't feed other calculations

**The financial calculation chain:**
```
Asset Cost, Tax, Admin, Risk Recovery, Salvage Value ($)
    ↓
Lease Base = Asset Cost + Tax + Admin + Risk - Salvage Value ($)
    ↓ [SEPARATE PATH]
start_date + term_months + salvage_periods
    ↓
payment_months = term_months - salvage_periods
    ↓
Loop periods 1 to payment_months
    ├→ Due date = start_date + period_number (day-of-month arithmetic)
    ├→ Installment = Lease Base / payment_months
    ├→ Interest = Outstanding Balance × (rate / 12)
    ├→ Amortization = Installment - Interest
    └→ Balance reduction
```

**Assessment - what operators should see:**
| Operator Display | Internal Term | Comments |
|---|---|---|
| Start Date | start_date | Anchor for payment dates |
| Asset Lifespan (months) | term_months | Raw contract life |
| Invoiced Periods | payment_months | **This is the count that matters for billing** |
| Term to Maturity (countdown) | payment_months - installments_paid | Remaining invoice count |
| Salvage Periods | salvage_periods | **Probably hidden; operators don't need to know this** |

**Recommendation - eliminate conceptual overhead:**

1. **Keep in data layer:** `term_months`, `salvage_periods` (needed for precision and flexibility)
2. **Never display `salvage_periods` to operators** (it's internal plumbing; they only need invoice count)
3. **Rename displays for clarity:**
   - Don't show "Term Months" alone (confusing if salvage exists)
   - Show "Invoiced Periods" (clear: this is how many times we bill)
   - Show "Term to Maturity" (countdown: how many invoices remain)
4. **No "Contract Time" concept** — `start_date` is the anchor, `payment_months` is the count, `final_month` is the result

**Question for you:** Should `salvage_periods` default to `0` in assets.csv, and only be populated when there's an actual salvage scenario? That way it's invisible in the common case.

[assistant.message]
Perfect sense. Yes, absolutely:

**Assets sheet columns — all user inputs at procurement time:**
- `term_months` = Asset's real lifespan (e.g., 24 months)
- `salvage_periods` = When salvage recovery kicks in (e.g., 1 = last period is salvage, no payment invoice)
- Calculation downstream: `payment_months = term_months - salvage_periods` = Invoice count (e.g., 23)

**Why it's correct:**
- Procurement team decides upfront: "This asset will live 24 months; we'll recover salvage in month 24 (non-invoiced)"
- That decision locks in the payment structure: 23 invoices, not 24
- The salvage dollar amount affects Lease Base; the salvage periods count affects invoice count
- Both are business decisions, both belong on the assets sheet

**Operator view then:**
- Operators inherit the structure: "This asset invoices 23 times" (payment_months)
- They monitor "Term to Maturity" = remaining invoices
- They don't need to see `salvage_periods` explicitly (it's baked into the payment_months they see)
- But it must exist in the data because it's a real business parameter

**Assessment: YES, keep current model:**
1. `term_months` (required input) — asset lifespan
2. `salvage_periods` (required input) — procurement decision on salvage timing
3. `payment_months` (calculated) — operational invoice count

Ready for **Point 3: Asset Start Month Business Rule** — the question about whether start_date (6/25) anchors first due date (7/25), or if we need a different model?

[user.message]
lets think about it, start date equates to that the asset is "delivered to the property at that date" therefore initiating the lease term. If daily wrapper is run before that date, it should not show as an active asset yet since its not to be invoiced yet. That is a good time to make that definition, does it make sense? weather  an asset is not active yet despite its planned, but still not "on lease", or its finished its invoicing an its balance is still pending

[assistant.message]
Yes, it makes perfect sense. And it directly connects to the **lifecycle status model**. Let me map what you're describing:

**Three distinct lifecycle states:**

| State | Condition | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Scheduled** | `as_of < start_date` | Asset procured, contract signed, not yet delivered/active |
| **Active** | `as_of >= start_date AND balance > 0` | On lease, generating invoices |
| **Matured** | `balance == 0 OR as_of >= final_month` | Lease complete, no more invoicing |

**Current model gap:**
Right now the system only computes `active` or `inactive`. It does NOT distinguish between:
- "inactive because it hasn't started yet" (scheduled)
- "inactive because it finished" (matured)

Both show as `inactive` today, which is misleading. A scheduled asset is fundamentally different from a matured one.

**Downstream implications:**

1. **Daily run**: If `as_of < start_date` → asset appears as `scheduled`, not shown in invoice-eligible pool
2. **Invoice run**: Only processes `active` assets (already the case), but now the status label is meaningful
3. **Workbook**: Inventory tab shows the correct state for operator clarity
4. **Balance distinction**: A matured asset might still carry a residual balance (rounding, salvage scenario) — worth defining whether that's a `matured` or `overage` state

**The key question for you to decide:**
- Should `scheduled` assets appear in the daily report at all (as a "coming soon" list), or be completely silent until start_date arrives?

**My recommendation:**
- Show them in daily run under a "Scheduled" section — operators can see what's coming
- Exclude them entirely from invoice calculations
- Matured assets stay visible in workbook (full history) but clearly labeled

Does this framing match your business intent? Ready to implement once you confirm.

**Point 4: Start-Date Semantics & Rate Alignment**

Three sub-questions:

1. **Invoicing period alignment** — When a contract starts on, say, June 25, the first invoice is due July 25, the second August 25, etc. The day-of-month from `start_date` anchors all future due dates. Is this the correct business behavior?

2. **Bank rate selection** — The system picks the most recent rate with `effective_date <= target month`. If rates change mid-lease, future invoices automatically pick up the new rate, but already-posted rows keep the rate at the time of posting. Is this the intended behavior?

3. **Last period / maturity logic** — At the final installment, does the balance correctly reach zero? Is there a rounding or salvage-value scenario where the last period behaves differently (e.g., smaller/larger final payment to close out the balance)?

Want to assess all three, or focus on one in particular?

[assistant.message]
Excellent set of modifications. Here is the suggested fix plan, one by one, with no code yet.

**1) Runner identity: Ana/Andres**
- Enforce operator as a controlled value, not free text.
- Accept only two canonical names: Ana and Andres.
- Normalize input case and spacing so ANA, ana, Andrés, andres all map to canonical output.
- Store only canonical names in manifests and outputs for clean audit trails.
- If value is outside allowed names, stop run with a clear error message.

Recommended behavior:
- Daily, invoice, and month-end all require one of those two names.
- Optional convenience: default can be inferred from local machine user, but still validated against Ana/Andres.

**2) Rate convention: nominal monthly instead of effective monthly compounding**
- Replace effective conversion with nominal monthly conversion.
- Current approach: monthly rate from compounding transform.
- New approach: monthly rate equals annual rate divided by 12.
- For lease rate:
  - monthly bank rate = bank APR / 12
  - monthly NIM rate = NIM annual / 12
  - monthly lease rate = monthly bank rate + monthly NIM rate
- Keep posted history locked as-is.
- Recompute only open/future rows with the new method.

Why this is right:
- It matches the bank’s revolving line convention for monthly interest on outstanding balance.

**3) Bank payable should be based on asset cost (not lease base)**
- Keep current lease schedule unchanged for commercial lease financials.
- Add a second, parallel bank-loan component per asset:
  - Principal base = asset cost only
  - Interest rate = bank APR monthly nominal (APR/12)
  - Same due-date timeline and payment periods unless business says otherwise
- Compute separate monthly bank components:
  - bank opening balance
  - bank interest
  - bank principal amortization
  - bank ending balance
- Bank payable monthly total should be sum of bank interest + bank principal for that cycle.
- Lease installment and lease balance remain for customer-facing economics and margin analysis.

Why this is right:
- Lease base includes tax/admin/risk/salvage effects for lease pricing.
- Bank debt finances only the asset purchase principal.

**Data model suggestion for point 3**
- Keep existing lease columns.
- Add bank-specific columns alongside them, not replacing them.
- This preserves transparency between:
  - customer economics (lease)
  - funding economics (bank)

**Control checks to include after implementation**
- For each asset, cumulative bank principal amortization equals asset cost at maturity (subject to cent rounding).
- Final bank balance reaches zero at last period.
- Posted months remain immutable.
- Bank payable file reflects bank component only, while invoice files keep lease amounts.

**Suggested rollout order**
1. Operator allowlist (low risk)
2. Nominal rate conversion for open periods
3. Add parallel bank component and switch bank payable source to it
4. Run one full rehearsal month and reconcile totals

If you want, next step I can draft the exact field-level specification for the new bank component so you approve naming before we implement.

[user.message]
in the class, why run daily for two months but only month close the first month?

[assistant.message]
Great question. It should not be interpreted as “close only the first month.”

What happened is the class sequence was arranged to show carry-forward behavior across cycles, but operationally the standard pattern is:

1. Daily check for Month N  
2. Invoice Month N  
3. Close Month N  
4. Daily check for Month N+1  
5. Invoice Month N+1  
6. Close Month N+1

So if the deck looked like daily for two months before a close, that is a training-order artifact, not the production rule.

For your policy (invoice date aligned with month-end date), the clean class flow should close each month immediately after invoicing that same month.

[user.message]
is there any changes on the calculations when running on those different sequences?

[assistant.message]
Short answer: normally no, the core invoice math should be the same if you execute the same month’s invoice and month-end once each.

What changes with sequence:
1. Daily outputs can differ because they are point-in-time previews.
2. Control status differs:
- If month-end is already done, that month is locked.
- If not, corrections are still operationally possible.
3. Bank payable timing differs only in when it is recorded, not in the intended month label.

So:
- Invoice calculations for July and August should not change just because you inserted an August daily preview before July close.
- The main impact is operational clarity and risk control, not financial formula differences.

Best practice remains:
1. Daily
2. Invoice
3. Month-end
for each month in order, to avoid confusion and accidental back-posting behavior.

[user.message]
the rates on the assets is expected in APR/12 month? (this is the new example set by the way)

[assistant.message]
Short answer: **No**.  
The value in assets/rates should still be **annual APR (decimal)**, not monthly APR/12 input.

How V1 now works:
1. You enter annual APR in the file.
2. Program converts internally to nominal monthly using:
   $$
   R_m = \frac{APR}{12}
   $$
3. Interest each month is calculated with that monthly nominal rate over opening balance.

Important check for your new dataset:
- If you intended **5.54% annual**, enter `0.0554`.
- If you enter `0.554`, the system reads it as **55.4% annual** (very high), then monthly nominal becomes about `0.04617` (4.617% per month).

So your question is exactly right to ask. With your screenshot, I would verify whether those `0.554` / `0.552` / `0.551` values are meant to be `0.0554` / `0.0552` / `0.0551`.

[user.message]
if rate is input on the rates, is it needed on the asset file?

[assistant.message]
Great question. In the current V1 logic, yes, it is still needed in both places for safety.

How it works now:
1. The monthly rate is taken from [ALC/rates.csv](ALC/rates.csv) when there is an effective rate for that month.
2. The rate in [ALC/assets.csv](ALC/assets.csv) is used as a fallback default if no applicable row exists yet in rates.csv for that period.
3. This also allows asset-level base-rate behavior if needed.

So operationally:
- Primary source: rates.csv
- Backup source: assets.csv

If you want, we can simplify in V2 so assets.csv no longer carries bank rate and the system requires rates.csv coverage for all months.

recommendation?

Recommendation: move to **single source of truth = `rates.csv`**, and keep asset-level rate only as temporary fallback during transition.

Best practice for your operation now:

2. Keep `bank_rate_annual` in assets only for backward compatibility.
3. Add a validation rule in next update:
- If no applicable rate exists in `rates.csv` for a required month, stop with clear error.
- Do not silently fall back once transition is complete.

Why this is better:
1. Prevents conflicting rates between files.
2. Reduces operator confusion and data-entry risk.
3. Improves auditability: all rate changes are in one timeline table.

Practical path:
1. **V1 testing now**: keep current behavior (safe fallback).
2. **V1.1**: warning when asset fallback is used.
3. **V2**: make rates.csv mandatory and remove fallback logic.

[user.message]
Apr has 30 days, even if run daily with 31 as invoicing, when will the reminder (I-1) will show up?

[assistant.message]
Great question.

With billing day set to 31, April clamps to April 30 (because April has only 30 days).  
So:

1. Billing date for April cycle = 2026-04-30
2. Reminder I-1 (invoice due tomorrow) appears on 2026-04-29 daily run.
3. Reminder “due today” appears on 2026-04-30 daily run.

So for April:
- Run `daily ... 2026-04-29 31` → I-1 reminder
- Run `daily ... 2026-04-30 31` → due-today reminder

[user.message]

[assistant.message]
Yes, exactly.

If you run Daily with:
- as-of date = April 29
- billing day = 31

the system first resolves billing day 31 for April to April 30 (clamped), then checks reminders.  
Since April 29 is one day before April 30, I-1 reminder triggers.

So reminder logic is based on the resolved billing date for that month, not the literal number 31.

[user.message]
its working now, It was my mistake. Question, is there any use at all for the asset file, the column status? real status shows in the workbook instead. asses

[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]


[assistant.message]
The `status` column in assets.csv **is used as a guard condition**, but only in one place:

```python
elif a.status == "active" and m["balance"] > 0:
    lifecycle_status = "active"
```

Here's how it works:
- **If you mark an asset as "active" in the CSV** and it still has a balance → it shows as "active"
- **Otherwise** (or if marked anything other than "active" in CSV) → it shows as "matured"
- **Dates override everything**: if current date < start_date → always "scheduled"

So the CSV `status` column is essentially a **manual cutoff flag**. If you want to mark an asset as "done invoicing" (matured) even though it mathematically still has a balance, you'd change it from "active" to anything else.

**Is it useful?** Rarely in practice. Most of the time, the computed status based on dates and balance is correct. The only scenario where you'd use it is if:
- An asset needs to stop invoicing early for business reasons
- You want to mark it "matured" manually without waiting for the balance to hit zero

If you never use this feature, you could simplify the code to remove it and just compute status from dates + balance. Want me to clean that up?

[assistant.message]
Two separate questions — both good ones.

**Q1: Why does Inventory show 6.65% Bank APR?**

That column shows `bank_rate_annual` from **assets.csv** — the asset's configured default rate. It's a static attribute, not a period-specific rate. The actual period-by-period calculations (both lease col C and bank col J-M) use the **rates.csv** monthly lookup. So:

| Where | Rate shown | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Inventory "Bank APR" | 6.65% | Asset's default rate from assets.csv |
| Asset tab col C period 1 | 6.63% | Apr 1 rate from rates.csv (overrides default) |
| Bank interest period 1 calc | 6.634%/12 | Same Apr 1 rate, applied to asset_value |

Both are correct — they represent different things.

---

**Q2: Is bank_payable 1117.90 the sum of all bank installments?**

Yes exactly:

$$\$1{,}117.90 = \sum_{\text{23 active assets}} \text{Bank Installment (col J, period 1)}$$

Which breaks down as:

$$= \underbrace{\$258.19}_{\text{sum col K (bank interest)}} + \underbrace{\$859.71}_{\text{sum col L (bank principal)}}$$

---

Also worth noting from your May daily output: `billing_date: 2026-06-01` — that's the weekend shift working correctly (May 31 is a Saturday → pushed to Monday June 1), but the cycle is still owned by **May 2026**. And still 23 invoice lines even though 25 are now active, because A1024/A1025 (started May 19) have their first payment due June 19 — outside the May billing window.

[user.message]
good, thank you. Now, brainstorm...initial assets start date is 3/3/26, those turn one month on 4/3/26, but are invoiced only on 4/30/26; almost 2 months have passed acruing for bank interests, but only one month invoice to the property lease. If keep invoicing at the end of the month, shouldn't we better invoiced actual days (e.g. 30-3=27 days by 3/31/26)? so each month matches exactly the bank acrual to the lease?

[assistant.message]
Good brainstorm. Let me lay out what's actually happening vs what you're proposing.

---

**Current system — what the periods actually are:**

```
Mar 3 ──────────────── Apr 3 ──────────── Apr 30
 │        Period 1              │            │
 │   (bank accrues here)        │            │
 │   (lease accrues here)       │            │
 │                         Both due      Invoice
 │                         Apr 3 →      collected
 │                         (bank)        Apr 30
```

Bank period 1 and lease period 1 **cover the exact same 31 days** (Mar 3 → Apr 3). They're aligned on accrual. The only mismatch is **cash collection timing**: bank expects payment Apr 3, you collect from property Apr 30 — 27 days of float the company absorbs.

---

**Your proposal — stub period billing:**

```
Mar 3 ──── Mar 31 ──────────── Apr 30 ──── May 31
   28 days    │   full month       │   full month
   partial    │                    │
   invoice    │                    │
```

This would mean:
- **First invoice**: 28/31 of a full installment (actual days Mar 3→Mar 31)
- **All subsequent**: full month installments at month-end
- **Bank**: still accrues full month Mar 3→Apr 3 regardless

---

**The problem with pro-rating:**

Even with pro-rating, the bank doesn't change its cycle — it still compounds monthly from Mar 3. So:

| Period | Bank accrual | Lease (pro-rated) |
|---|---|---|
| Mar | Mar 3–Apr 3 (31 days) | Mar 3–Mar 31 (28 days) |
| Apr | Apr 3–May 3 (30 days) | Mar 31–Apr 30 (30 days) |

They're now *more* misaligned, not less — the bank's month and the calendar month are different periods.

---

**Honest recommendation:**

The current design is **already correct on accruals**. The real issue is purely **cash flow**: you pay the bank before you collect from the property. Two cleaner fixes for that:

1. **Move billing to the 3rd** (invoice on the same day the lease period turns) — eliminates the float entirely
2. **Accept the float as policy** — normal in equipment leasing; the 27-day gap is part of your liquidity planning

Pro-rating adds billing complexity and doesn't fix the underlying timing gap. Want to explore option 1 (billing on payment due date instead of month-end)?

Best low-maintenance path is a hybrid:

1. Microsoft 365-native agent for user interaction
2. One deterministic Python service for calculations

That gives you easy adoption for finance users and keeps the math logic centralized and controlled.

**Recommended Architecture**
1. Front door: Copilot Studio agent in Teams
2. Workflow: Power Automate cloud flow
3. Data store: SharePoint document library + SharePoint Lists
4. Deterministic engine: your Python tool hosted once (Azure Function or Container App)
5. Approval: Teams Approvals or Outlook approval flow
6. Output: generated Excel/CSV/PDF/email draft saved to SharePoint and posted to Teams

Why this is simplest:
- Users stay inside Teams/Outlook
- No one runs scripts locally
- You maintain one Python runtime, not many desktops
- Native approvals/audit trail in M365
- Easy RBAC with Entra ID groups

**What the agent should do (minimal viable)**
1. Intake:
Collect period, billing day, operator, input file versions
2. Validate:
Check required files exist in SharePoint and schema is valid
3. Run deterministic tool:
Call Python API with strict payload
4. Return results:
Show summary totals + attach draft outputs
5. Ask approval:
Approve or reject with comments
6. Publish:
On approval, save final files, log manifest, send final email/report draft

**Keep it deterministic (important)**
1. Agent never computes finance numbers itself
2. Agent only orchestrates and explains
3. Python service is single source of truth for numbers
4. Every run writes a manifest with:
input hashes, version, timestamp, approver, outputs

**Option comparison (quick)**
1. Copilot Studio + Power Automate + Python API (recommended):
Lowest user friction, good governance, medium setup
2. Standalone Streamlit/Flask app:
Fast to build, but weaker M365 approvals/integration and more custom auth work
3. Claude-only assistant:
Not ideal for your users because disconnected from enterprise workflows and access

[user.message]
should it run only. monthly? or daily as is designed for? yes, let see that MVP

[assistant.message]
Great question. Short answer: **both**, but with different intent.

1. **Daily mode**: monitoring, readiness checks, reminders, exception detection  
2. **Monthly mode**: the financial “posting” actions (invoice and month-end) with approval gates

If you force monthly-only, you lose early-warning value. If you force daily-only without gating, you risk accidental postings. So the best MVP is a **dual-mode agent**.

**Recommended Operating Model**
1. Daily at 8:00 AM local:
Run a non-posting check and send “what’s due next” summary.
2. On billing execution day:
Run invoice workflow only after user confirms.
3. Immediately after invoice:
Run month-end workflow only after second confirmation.
4. Any day:
Allow ad-hoc “simulate” run (no writes) for what-if review.

**Why this matches your tool**
1. Your current design already separates daily preview vs invoice vs month-end.  
2. Finance users get guided steps, not raw scripts.  
3. Deterministic Python stays the only calculator; agent only orchestrates.

## MVP You Can Build Fast
**Channel**: Teams agent (Copilot Studio)  
**Orchestration**: Power Automate  
**Compute**: One Python API endpoint set (Azure Function or Container App)  
**Storage**: SharePoint library + list for run logs  
**Approval**: Teams/Outlook approval actions

### MVP Flows
1. **Flow A: Daily Portfolio Check (read-only)**
- Trigger: scheduled daily
- Action: call Python “daily preview” endpoint
- Output: Teams card with totals, upcoming invoice day, data quality flags
- No posting, no file mutation

2. **Flow B: Invoice Run (controlled write)**
- Trigger: user clicks “Run Invoice for Month”
- Agent asks: month, billing day, operator, “proceed?”
- Action: call Python invoice endpoint
- Output: draft artifacts in SharePoint + summary card
- Approval: finance approver accepts/rejects
- On approve: mark run as official and notify

3. **Flow C: Month-End Close (controlled write)**
- Trigger: only after approved invoice run
- Action: call bank-payable + close-period endpoint
- Output: bank payable summary, closed period record, workbook refresh
- Approval: second gate before final close flag

### Guardrails (must-have in MVP)
1. “Simulation” vs “Post” mode flag
2. Duplicate-run protection per month
3. Mandatory operator and approver identity capture
4. Immutable run manifest with input hashes
5. Role-based permissions:
- Analyst can simulate + request run
- Approver can post + close

## Should It Run Monthly or Daily?
Use this policy:
1. **Daily**: always run monitoring automatically
2. **Monthly posting**: run only by explicit human action + approval
3. **Month-end close**: never automatic in MVP

That gives low maintenance and high control.

## MVP Conversation UX (Teams agent)
1. “Run today’s check”
2. “Prepare April invoice”
3. “Show me differences vs last month”
4. “Request approval to post invoice”
5. “Close April period”

1. Week 1: wrap existing Python commands as API endpoints
2. Week 2: build Daily and Invoice flows in Power Automate
3. Week 3: add approvals + month-end close flow
4. Week 4: pilot with 2 users and tighten prompts/guards

Describe this project MVP (all 5 steps), and suggest name. 

[assistant.message]
Recommended MVP name: ALC FlowPilot

It signals two core ideas:
1. ALC-specific deterministic finance engine
2. Guided, low-friction workflow for non-technical users

MVP in 5 steps

1. Intake and context capture
- User starts from Teams and tells the agent the run intent: daily check, invoice month, or month-end close.
- Agent collects required inputs: month or as-of date, billing day, operator, and confirms input files are present.
- Outcome: clean, validated request package.

2. Deterministic run execution
- Agent calls the Python API endpoint that maps directly to the existing command logic.
- No finance math is done by the agent itself.
- Outcome: reproducible run with manifest ID, totals, and generated artifacts.

3. Result packaging and explanation
- Agent returns a plain-language summary for finance users:
  - portfolio totals
  - invoice lines and amount
  - bank interest, bank principal, bank payable
  - warnings or exceptions
- Outcome: user understands what happened without reading logs.

4. Human approval gate
- For posting actions (invoice and month-end), the agent creates an approval request in M365 flow.
- Approver can approve or reject with comments.
- Outcome: controlled governance and clear responsibility trail.

5. Publish, archive, and audit trail
- Approved outputs are stored in SharePoint, with links posted in Teams.
- Run metadata is written to a tracking list: who ran, who approved, when, which inputs, which outputs.
- Outcome: operational completion plus audit-ready traceability.

Why this is the right MVP
1. Easy for finance users: no script execution
2. Low maintenance: one Python logic base, thin orchestration layer
3. Safe: deterministic engine plus approval gates
4. Enterprise fit: native to Teams, SharePoint, and Microsoft approvals

Good. Week 1 is the right place to reset, because it is the technical foundation.

**Week 1 objective**
Take the existing ALC Python tool and expose it as a small deterministic API so later a Microsoft 365 agent can call it safely.

**Week 1 in plain terms**
Right now:
- a user or operator runs shell scripts

After Week 1:
- a system can call endpoints like:
  - `daily-preview`
  - `invoice`
  - `month-end`
  - `one-pager`

The finance math does not move and does not change. Only the entry point changes.

**What Week 1 is really building**
1. A thin API wrapper around the current engine
2. Input validation
3. Standard JSON responses
4. Error handling
5. Run tracking with manifest IDs

**The 5 concrete Week 1 steps**
1. Define the endpoint contract
- decide exact routes, payloads, and response format
- example:
  - `POST /api/v1/runs/daily-preview`
  - `POST /api/v1/runs/invoice`
  - `POST /api/v1/runs/month-end`
  - `POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager`
  - `GET /api/v1/health`

2. Build the API wrapper
- use a lightweight Python web framework
- the API should call the same internal logic the scripts use now
- no duplication of financial formulas

3. Add validation and protections
- validate dates, month format, billing day, operator
- reject bad payloads early
- block duplicate month-end close if already closed

4. Return structured outputs
- every response should include:
  - success/failure
  - run_id
  - summary totals
  - output file locations
  - warnings/errors

5. Smoke test the full sequence
- health
- daily preview
- invoice
- month-end
- one-pager
- confirm totals match current script behavior

**Simplest technical recommendation for Week 1**
Use **FastAPI**.

Why:
1. Very low maintenance
2. Native JSON API
3. Easy request validation
4. Easy Swagger docs for IT
5. Good fit for Azure Function wrapper later or Container App hosting

**Suggested Week 1 architecture**
- existing engine:
  - [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py)
- new API module:
  - `app.py` or `api.py`
- optional service layer:
  - `services/runs.py`
- no changes to business math unless required for clean function reuse

**What IT should understand**
Week 1 is not “build the agent.”
Week 1 is:
- make the current tool callable by systems
- keep it deterministic
- prepare it for M365 orchestration later

**What success looks like at the end of Week 1**
An authenticated HTTP call can do this:
1. receive a valid payload
2. run the same logic as today
3. return the same totals as today
4. expose file outputs and run manifest
5. fail safely when inputs are wrong

[assistant.message]
Good. Then Step 1 should be very narrow: understand what an API is in this project, not build everything at once.

**Step 1: What the API is here**

In your case, the API is just a controlled front door to the Python tool.

Today:
- you run a command like `./scripts/op_daily.sh ana 2026-04-29 31`

With an API:
- another system sends a request like:
```json
{
  "operator": "ana",
  "as_of": "2026-04-29",
  "billing_day": 31
}
```

And the API responds with structured output like:
```json
{
  "success": true,
  "command": "daily-preview",
  "run_id": "20260730T101500-ab12cd34",
  "summary": {
    "total_assets": 27,
    "active_assets": 23,
    "invoice_amount_due": 1270.58
  }
}
```

So the API is **not a new calculator**.
It is just a translator between:
1. external request
2. your existing Python logic
3. structured response

**Why you need it**
Because Microsoft tools, agents, and workflows are much better at calling:
- HTTP endpoints
than at:
- SSH-ing into a machine
- running shell scripts manually
- parsing terminal text

**What changes in Week 1**
Not the math.
Only the entry point.

Before:
- shell wrapper -> Python tool -> files/output

After:
- API request -> Python tool -> JSON + files/output

**The simplest mental model**
Think of the API as a receptionist:
1. receives a request
2. checks if it is valid
3. tells your Python engine what to do
4. returns the result in a clean format

**What Step 1 should produce**
By the end of Step 1, you should be clear on these 4 things:
1. which commands need endpoints
2. what inputs each one needs
3. what outputs each one returns
4. that the finance logic stays exactly where it is

For your project, the first endpoint list is:

1. `daily-preview`
2. `invoice`
3. `month-end`
4. `one-pager`
5. `health`

**Your simplest technical stack**
Use:
- **FastAPI** for the API
- your existing [ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py](ALC/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py) as the engine

That is the easiest modern Python path.

**Step 2, when you're ready**
Define one endpoint only:
- `POST /api/v1/runs/daily-preview`

That is the best first API because:
1. it is read-only
2. lowest risk
3. easiest to test
4. proves the architecture

If you want, I can now give you **Step 2 only**:
the exact files to create and the minimal FastAPI scaffold for just `daily-preview`.

[assistant.message]
Step 2: define the first endpoint only.

**Endpoint**
`POST /api/v1/runs/daily-preview`

**Why start here**
1. Read-only
2. Lowest risk
3. Uses the current engine without posting files
4. Good proof that API wrapping works

**Input**
```json
{
  "operator": "ana",
  "as_of": "2026-04-29",
  "billing_day": 31,
  "assets_path": "assets.csv",
  "rates_path": "rates.csv"
}
```

**Validation**
1. `operator`: required, non-empty
2. `as_of`: required, `YYYY-MM-DD`
3. `billing_day`: integer `1-31`
4. `assets_path`: optional, default `assets.csv`
5. `rates_path`: optional, default `rates.csv`

**Output**
```json
{
  "success": true,
  "command": "daily-preview",
  "run_id": "20260730T101500-ab12cd34",
  "summary": {
    "total_assets": 27,
    "scheduled_assets": 4,
    "active_assets": 23,
    "matured_assets": 0,
    "asset_value_total": 57202.8,
```

[user.message]
Good, that se have clear. The next step would be to build it, and since it seems it will connect agent (copilot studio) with where the python script lives as engine, that architecture is what I wanted to understand

[assistant.message]
Correct. The next step is to build the API layer, and the architecture you want to understand is the connection path between three parts:

1. user-facing agent
2. orchestration/integration layer
3. deterministic Python engine

**Target architecture**
```text
Finance User
   |
   v
Copilot Studio Agent
   |
   v
Power Automate or direct custom connector
   |
   v
HTTP API
   |
   v
Python engine (alc_item_sheet_tracker.py)
   |
   +--> generated files
   +--> manifests
   +--> SharePoint / storage
```

**What each layer does**

1. **Copilot Studio**
- collects inputs from the finance user
- asks confirmations
- explains outputs in plain language
- does not calculate financials

2. **Power Automate or Custom Connector**
- sends structured request to the API
- handles approval flow
- stores or routes outputs
- connects naturally to M365 services

3. **API layer**
- validates request
- calls Python engine functions
- catches errors
- returns structured JSON
- this is the bridge between agent and engine

4. **Python engine**
- remains the source of truth
- performs all deterministic calculations
- writes outputs and manifests

**Why this is the right architecture**
1. Lowest maintenance
2. Finance users stay in Microsoft tools
3. Core math remains centralized
4. Agent can be changed later without rewriting finance logic
5. Safer than letting the agent compute anything itself

**Simplest deployment choices**
Best first option:
1. host Python API on **Azure App Service** or **Azure Container Apps**
2. expose a small HTTPS endpoint
3. connect Copilot Studio through Power Automate/custom connector

That is simpler than trying to let Copilot Studio run local scripts directly.

**Important boundary**
The agent should never talk to shell scripts on a laptop or desktop.
It should talk to:
- a stable API
- on a known host
- with controlled auth
- with controlled file paths

**So the architecture answer in one sentence**
Copilot Studio should call an HTTP API, and that API should call the Python engine that lives on a managed host, not on a user machine.

**What this means for Week 1**
Week 1 builds only this boxed layer:

```text
Copilot/Power Automate
        |
        v
   [ Python API ]
        |
        v
alc_item_sheet_tracker.py
```

If you want, next step I can give you the **exact build order** for this architecture in 5 technical steps, starting from local development and ending at the point where Copilot Studio could call it.

[user.message]
recommend which to use, power automate or custom connector. pro vs cons. concise. Also, agent lives in copilot studio (platform), where does the aPI and the engine live ?

[assistant.message]
Recommendation: start with Power Automate first, add a Custom Connector only when needed.

Why:
1. fastest MVP
2. built-in approvals, SharePoint, Teams, Outlook actions
3. lower maintenance for non-dev admins

Power Automate vs Custom Connector

1. Power Automate
Pros:
1. quickest to stand up
2. strong M365 integration out of the box
3. easy human approval steps
4. good for orchestration and audit trails

Cons:
1. less elegant API contract management
2. harder to reuse as a clean “productized” API surface
3. flow complexity can grow over time

2. Custom Connector
Pros:
1. clean reusable API interface for Copilot Studio and flows
2. better long-term governance/versioning
3. easier to standardize multiple endpoints

Cons:
1. more setup effort upfront
2. requires stronger API discipline (OpenAPI/auth/error schema)
3. still needs Power Automate (or equivalent) for rich approval workflows

Where API and engine should live

1. Copilot Studio Agent
Lives in Microsoft Copilot Studio (cloud, user-facing conversation layer)

2. API
Lives in Azure as a hosted HTTP service:
1. Azure App Service (simple and solid)
2. Azure Container Apps (good if containerized)
3. Azure Functions (good for event-style/light workloads)

3. Python Engine
Lives with the API on the same Azure workload (same app/container), not on user laptops.

4. Data/artifacts
Inputs/outputs in SharePoint/OneDrive or Azure Storage, depending on IT policy.

Best MVP stack
1. Copilot Studio agent
2. Power Automate flow
3. Azure-hosted FastAPI service wrapping your Python engine
4. SharePoint for files + approvals in Teams/Outlook

This gives lowest friction now and a clean path to scale later.

Recommendation:

Power Automate:
Pros:

- Easier for non-developers to maintain.
Cons:
- Flows can become complex at scale.
- API surface is less standardized.
- Harder long-term version control across many endpoints.

Custom Connector:
Pros:
- Clean, reusable API contract.
- Better long-term governance and versioning.
- Easier reuse across Copilot Studio and multiple flows.
Cons:
- More setup effort upfront.
- Requires stronger API spec/auth discipline.
- Still usually paired with Power Automate for approvals and orchestration.

Where components live:
- Agent: Copilot Studio (Microsoft cloud).
- API: Azure-hosted Python web service (App Service or Container Apps).
- Engine: same Azure app/container as the API, calling your deterministic Python logic.
- Files/artifacts: SharePoint/OneDrive or Azure Storage (per IT policy).

Best MVP stack:
- Copilot Studio + Power Automate + Azure-hosted FastAPI + SharePoint.

[user.message]
explain 1 briefly

[assistant.message]
I’ll map this to the least disruptive approach: keep the existing historical ledger as locked baseline data, then start the new engine from a fresh as-of snapshot instead of trying to rewrite prior months.Yes — the simplest path is not “rewrite all history,” but “freeze legacy history and restart from a single opening snapshot.”

The easiest model is:

- Keep all old closed periods as historical actuals.
- Do not recalculate settled months.
- Start the new engine from a go-live date, using the baseline balances as the opening balances.
- Continue forward from there with normal daily/invoice/month-end logic.

Recommended approach

1. Freeze legacy history
- Treat all current historical ledger rows as locked.
- Do not overwrite them.
- Keep them as “actuals” in a separate historical folder or dataset.
- The engine should only use those rows for prior periods and never re-run them.

2. Define a go-live date
- Example: 2026-08-31 or 2026-09-01.
- This is the first date the new engine starts fresh.

3. Build one opening snapshot file
Create a file like:
- opening_balances.csv
with one row per asset, containing:
- asset_id
- property_id
- asset_cost
- lease_balance_as_of_go_live
- bank_balance_as_of_go_live
- next_due_date
- remaining_months
- effective_rate
- status
- last_closed_period

This is the “restart state.”

4. Seed the engine from that snapshot
At go-live, the engine should:
- read opening_balances.csv
- ignore historical rows before that date
- start amortization from the current outstanding lease and bank balances
- continue moving forward from there

This is the key idea: the new engine is not recalculating 2026-04 to 2026-08. It starts from the correct current balances at the start of the new cycle.

5. Keep a clear distinction
Use two modes:
- legacy_mode: read-only historical ledger
- live_mode: current projection engine from go-live forward

This prevents confusion between “what actually happened” and “what is being projected now.”

Why this is the easiest and safest
- No historical rewrite
- No risk of breaking settled general ledger entries
- Lets the team keep old numbers for audit and accounting
- Gives a clean restart for forward-looking operations
- Easy to explain to finance and IT

Best practical implementation

Use a simple startup file:
- opening_balances.csv

Then add a small pre-check in the engine:
- if as_of >= go_live_date:
  - read opening snapshot
  - use those balances as the opening state
- else:
  - use historical ledger behavior

This is much easier than trying to reconstruct every past month exactly.

Important rule
The “baseline” should be treated as a business snapshot, not as a perfect recalculation. It is the opening place from which future months are calculated. That matches your requirement:
- keep old history
- preserve financial reality
- start fresh from current estimate

My recommendation
Use:
- “legacy locked” data for all historical periods
- “opening_snapshot” data for the restart
- “live projection” for all periods after go-live

This is the cleanest compromise between accuracy, auditability, and operational simplicity.

If you want, I can next help you define the exact columns for the opening_balances.csv file and the exact logic that the engine should follow when it sees a go-live snapshot.

the user runs microsoft laptop, she has already the ALC folder with the scripts and installed python on her computer; however, she has not been able to run wrapper 

[assistant.message]
I’m checking the wrapper script itself and the working directory assumptions so I can tell you why this particular Windows/macOS local setup fails and what the exact fix is.

[assistant.message]
## Why the wrapper fails on a Microsoft laptop

The issue is not the logic in the engine. It is the wrapper itself.

In [ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh](ALC/scripts/op_daily.sh), the script is written for bash, not for Windows CMD or PowerShell:

- it starts with `#!/usr/bin/env bash`
- it uses bash-specific syntax like `[[ ... ]]`
- it hardcodes a Unix Python path:
  - `/opt/anaconda3/bin/python`
- if that path does not exist, it falls back to `python3`

So on a Windows laptop, this fails because:

1. `.sh` files are not native Windows executables
2. `/opt/anaconda3/...` does not exist on Windows
3. PowerShell/CMD do not execute the bash wrapper the same way Linux/macOS does

## What to do

### Best simple option
Use one of these:

- WSL2
- Git Bash
- a bash-compatible shell

Then run from the ALC folder:

```bash
cd /path/to/ALC
bash ./scripts/op_daily.sh ana 2026-04-29 31
```

### If they want to stay in PowerShell
Then the shell script must be replaced or wrapped by a Windows-native command. For example:

```powershell
py -3 .\alc_item_sheet_tracker.py daily --operator ana --as-of 2026-04-29 --billing-day 31
```

That is the equivalent of the wrapper, without calling the `.sh` file.

## Most likely root cause
The wrapper was designed for a Unix-like environment and not for a normal Windows laptop. That is why it “looks installed” but still cannot run.

## Recommendation
For the user:
- use WSL2 or Git Bash
- or bypass the wrapper and call the Python engine directly

This is the cleanest minimal fix.

The design note proposes adding a **propose → approve → commit** control layer around invoice and month-end operations:

- Preview calculates results without writing anything.
- Approval is represented by a later commit call.
- Commit rechecks input file hashes before writing.
- If inputs changed, the commit is rejected and a new preview is required.
- Existing run manifests become the proposal records.
- Daily and one-pager operations remain single-call because they do not mutate ledger or closing state.
- The API would expose separate preview and commit endpoints for invoice and month-end runs.

The key architectural point is that financial calculations remain in the Python engine. The API only controls authorization and orchestration.

[user.message]
the objective is that if the script is run from azure by a copilot agent call, agent would not be able to write on asset or rates files without being explicitly allowed by the users after requesting prompt. This seem to go beyond that?

[assistant.message]
I’ll compare the design note’s scope with your narrower control objective and identify the minimum architecture needed.Yes. The design note goes beyond your stated objective.

Your objective is:

> A Copilot agent may read `assets.csv` and `rates.csv`, but it must not modify them unless the user explicitly approves the change.

That requires two controls:

1. **Technical permission boundary**
   - Agent/API identity has read-only access to `assets.csv` and `rates.csv`.
   - Only an approved administrative operation can obtain write access.

2. **Explicit approval workflow**
   - Agent proposes the requested change.
   - User approves it.
   - A controlled commit operation performs the write.

The design note instead creates a broader approval system for invoice and month-end operations, including:

- posted invoice ledger
- bank-payable totals
- closed periods
- workbook generation
- input hash validation
- proposal expiration
- preview and commit endpoints for financial runs

That is useful for preventing unapproved financial postings, but it is broader than protecting `assets.csv` and `rates.csv`.

The minimum design should be:

```text
Copilot agent
    |
    | read-only operations
    v
API / engine
    |
    | assets.csv and rates.csv cannot be written
    |
    | requested change
    v
Proposal returned to user
    |
    | explicit approval
    v
Authorized asset/rate update endpoint
    |
    v
Controlled write to assets.csv or rates.csv
```

Also, the existing `daily`, `invoice`, and `month-end` commands should remain unable to modify `assets.csv` or `rates.csv`. Their writes should be limited to generated outputs and operational ledgers.

So the design note should be narrowed or split:

- **Required now:** protect asset and rate inputs with read-only permissions plus explicit approval for changes.
- **Optional later:** propose/approve/commit for invoice posting, bank payable, and period closing.

The strongest implementation is to enforce read-only access at the Azure storage identity level, not only through prompts or agent instructions. A prompt can request approval, but it should never be the only security control.

The agent’s normal execution identity would have read-only access. The approved commit action would use a separate controlled path with write permission.

That does not necessarily mean the user needs a second username and password. Possible approval models are:

- **Teams approval:** user clicks an Approve button in an Adaptive Card.
- **Power Automate approval:** user approves through a Microsoft 365 approval action.
- **Entra ID step-up authentication:** user confirms with MFA for sensitive changes.
- **Separate approver role:** only designated finance users can approve writes.

The important distinction is:

- The agent cannot silently write.
- The user approves inside the normal Microsoft 365 workflow.
- The backend performs the write only after verifying that approval.
- The user never manually opens Azure Storage or edits the file directly.

For your case, the simplest MVP is:

```text
Copilot asks for confirmation
    -> Teams/Power Automate records approval
    -> API receives approved request
    -> API writes assets.csv or rates.csv
```

A second authentication factor is optional, but the approval must be represented by a trusted Microsoft 365 identity and enforced by the backend. A plain message such as “yes” should not by itself grant storage write access.

[user.message]
summarize and leave outputs guardrails for second version. draft 

[assistant.message]
I’ll draft a concise version-two guardrails note that preserves the current engine scope and separates input-edit controls from output-posting controls.# ALC Version 2 Guardrails Draft

## Objective

Allow the Copilot agent to operate the ALC engine while preventing unapproved changes to source data and financial outputs.

## Version 1 Boundary

The agent may:

- run daily reports
- calculate invoice previews
- generate one-pager workbooks
- read assets, rates, ledgers, and reports
- propose new assets or rate changes

The agent may not directly modify:

- `assets.csv`
- `rates.csv`

Any new entry or change requires explicit user approval through Teams or Power Automate.

## Version 2 Guardrails

### 1. Source-file protection

`assets.csv` and `rates.csv` remain read-only to the normal agent/API identity.

Changes must use controlled endpoints:

```text
POST /assets/propose
POST /assets/commit
POST /rates/propose
POST /rates/commit
```

The commit operation must verify:

- authenticated approver
- approval ID or correlation ID
- valid fields and data types
- duplicate asset IDs
- valid effective dates
- valid rate ranges
- unchanged source-file hash since proposal

### 2. Output protection

Financial outputs should also use controlled write operations.

The agent may preview:

- invoice lines
- invoice totals
- bank payable
- period-close impact

The agent may not commit these changes without approval:

- `posted_invoices.csv`
- `bank_payable.csv`
- `closed_periods.csv`
- final workbook publication

Proposed endpoints:

```text
POST /runs/invoice/preview
POST /runs/invoice/commit
POST /runs/month-end/preview
POST /runs/month-end/commit
```

### 3. Approval rules

Approval must happen through a trusted Microsoft 365 identity, such as:

- Teams approval card
- Power Automate approval
- designated finance approver
- Entra ID step-up authentication for sensitive changes

A plain text response such as “yes” should not independently grant write access.

### 4. Auditability

Every proposal and commit should record:

- run or proposal ID
- requesting user
- approving user
- timestamp
- original values
- proposed values
- resulting values
- source-file hashes
- affected output files
- success or rejection reason

### 5. Expiration and conflict handling

Proposals should expire after a defined period, such as 24 hours.

A commit must be rejected when:

- the proposal has expired
- the source file changed
- the approval is missing or invalid
- the requested record no longer matches
- validation fails

The user must create a new proposal after rejection.

## Recommended Sequence

### Version 1

- deterministic engine
- read-only API access
- daily and reporting operations
- preview-only invoice and month-end calculations
- controlled approval for asset and rate changes

### Version 2

- approval-controlled invoice posting
- approval-controlled bank payable updates
- approval-controlled period closing
- final workbook publication
- complete audit trail
- proposal expiration and conflict detection

This keeps Version 1 practical while reserving broader financial commit controls for Version 2.

[user.message]

[assistant.message]
I’ll outline the Azure storage layout and the Teams response pattern so originals remain untouched while each run produces a dated, user-visible copy.Yes. The Azure version can preserve the original files and publish dated output copies for Teams.

## Recommended Azure layout

Azure Blob Storage uses virtual folders. You can keep the originals in the same logical ALC area:

```text
alc/
  assets.csv
  rates.csv
  posted_invoices.csv
  closed_periods.csv
  ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx

  outputs/
    daily_2026-08-21.csv
    invoices_2026-08.csv
    one-pager_2026-08-21.xlsx
    bank_payable_2026-08.csv

  manifests/
    daily_<run_id>.json
    invoice_<run_id>.json
```

The original source files remain unchanged. The API:

1. reads the original Azure files
2. copies them to a temporary execution folder
3. runs the Python package
4. uploads generated outputs with dated names
5. records the run manifest
6. returns links to the agent
7. Copilot presents those links in Teams

## Example Teams response

```text
Daily report completed for 2026-08-21.

Files:
- Daily report
- One-pager workbook
- Run manifest

Open files:
[Daily report]
[One-pager workbook]
```

The links should be short-lived secure links, such as Azure Blob SAS URLs, or SharePoint links if the outputs are published there.

## Naming rule

Use the business date and a unique run ID:

```text
daily_2026-08-21_<run_id>.csv
invoices_2026-08-2026_<run_id>.csv
one-pager_2026-08-21_<run_id>.xlsx
```

For recurring outputs, the cleaner pattern is:

```text
outputs/2026/08/21/daily_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/21/one-pager_<run_id>.xlsx
```

This prevents one run from overwriting another.

## Important protection

The API identity should have:

- read access to original input files
- write access only to `outputs/` and `manifests/`
- no overwrite permission on original inputs

For source files, also enable:

- Blob versioning
- soft delete
- immutable retention or legal hold where appropriate
- hash recording in each manifest

## About changes to assets or rates

When an approved user changes an asset or rate, do not silently overwrite the original file. Create a dated controlled version:

```text
alc/
  rates.csv
  archive/
    rates_2026-08-20.csv
  outputs/
  manifests/
```

Or use a versioned database/table later.

The first Azure implementation can keep `assets.csv` and `rates.csv` as protected source files, while generated reports are copied into `outputs/` and surfaced in Teams. The Python package remains the calculation engine; the API handles Azure file movement, naming, permissions, and Teams links.

## V1 Scope

### Protected inputs

```text
inputs/assets.csv
inputs/rates.csv
```

The agent may read them, but may not directly write them.

### Generated outputs

```text
outputs/2026/08/21/daily_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/21/invoices_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/21/one-pager_<run_id>.xlsx
outputs/2026/08/21/bank-payable_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
```

Each execution creates a new dated copy. Existing outputs are not overwritten.

### V1 exclusions

Do not add approval controls yet for:

- invoice posting
- bank payable
- closed periods
- workbook publication

Those output guardrails become V2 after the basic Azure workflow is operating reliably.

## Azure Storage Layout

```text
alc-container/
  inputs/
    assets.csv
    rates.csv

  working/
    <run_id>/
      assets.csv
      rates.csv

  outputs/
    2026/
      08/
        21/
          daily_<run_id>.csv
          invoices_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
          one-pager_<run_id>.xlsx

  manifests/
    <run_id>.json

  archives/
    inputs/
      assets_<timestamp>.csv
      rates_<timestamp>.csv
```

The engine should run against a temporary `working/<run_id>/` directory. It should not directly modify the protected originals.

## Storage Permissions

Use separate identities or managed identities:

```text
Agent/API read identity:
  read inputs/
  write working/
  write outputs/
  write manifests/
  no direct write to inputs/

Approved input-update identity:
  read inputs/
  write inputs/
  write archives/
  write manifests/
```

The normal agent cannot bypass the API to update `assets.csv` or `rates.csv`.

## API Endpoints

### Read and calculation endpoints

```text
GET /api/v1/assets
GET /api/v1/rates

POST /api/v1/runs/daily
POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager
POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable
```

These endpoints:

1. download the current inputs
2. create a temporary run folder
3. execute the unchanged Python engine
4. upload dated outputs
5. write a manifest
6. return Teams-accessible file links

### Input proposal endpoints

```text
POST /api/v1/assets/propose
POST /api/v1/rates/propose
POST /api/v1/assets/approve
POST /api/v1/rates/approve
```

The proposal endpoint does not modify the input files.

## Input-Change Flow

```text
User:
  "Change the July bank rate to 6.6171%."

Agent:
  Read current rates.csv.
  Validate the date and rate.
  Create proposal.
  Show old value and new value.
  Ask for approval.

User:
  "Approve."

Approval service:
  Verify the authenticated Microsoft 365 user.
  Return approval ID.

API:
  Verify proposal ID.
  Verify approval ID.
  Re-read current rates.csv.
  Confirm it has not changed since proposal.
  Archive the current rates.csv.
  Write the approved new version.
  Record audit manifest.

Agent:
  Confirm the change and show the new effective date.
```

## Input Update Pseudocode

```python
def propose_rate_change(request, authenticated_user):
    current_rates = blob.read("inputs/rates.csv")

    validate_rate_date(request.effective_date)
    validate_rate_value(request.bank_rate_annual)

    old_value = find_rate(current_rates, request.effective_date)

    proposal = {
        "proposal_id": new_id(),
        "type": "rate_change",
        "requested_by": authenticated_user.id,
        "old_value": old_value,
        "new_value": request.bank_rate_annual,
        "effective_date": request.effective_date,
        "input_hash": hash(current_rates),
        "status": "pending_approval",
        "expires_at": now() + 24_hours,
    }

    blob.write_json("manifests/proposals/<proposal_id>.json", proposal)

    return proposal
```

```python
def approve_rate_change(proposal_id, approval):
    proposal = load_proposal(proposal_id)

    verify_m365_approval(approval)
    verify_approval_user_is_authorized(approval.user)
    verify_not_expired(proposal)

    current_rates = blob.read("inputs/rates.csv")

    if hash(current_rates) != proposal["input_hash"]:
        reject("INPUT_CHANGED")

    updated_rates = apply_rate_change(
        current_rates,
        proposal["effective_date"],
        proposal["new_value"],
    )

    archive(
        source="inputs/rates.csv",
        destination=f"archives/inputs/rates_{timestamp()}.csv",
    )

    blob.write("inputs/rates.csv", updated_rates)

    proposal["status"] = "committed"
    proposal["approved_by"] = approval.user
    proposal["approved_at"] = now()

    blob.write_json("manifests/proposals/<proposal_id>.json", proposal)

    return proposal
```

The asset add/change flow uses the same pattern:

```python
propose_asset_change()
approve_asset_change()
```

## Run Pseudocode

```python
def run_daily(request, user):
    run_id = new_id()
    run_folder = f"working/{run_id}"

    assets = blob.read("inputs/assets.csv")
    rates = blob.read("inputs/rates.csv")

    blob.write(f"{run_folder}/assets.csv", assets)
    blob.write(f"{run_folder}/rates.csv", rates)

    result = engine.run_daily(
        assets=f"{run_folder}/assets.csv",
        rates=f"{run_folder}/rates.csv",
        as_of=request.as_of,
    )

    output_path = (
        f"outputs/{request.as_of.year}/"
        f"{request.as_of.month:02d}/"
        f"{request.as_of.day:02d}/"
        f"daily_{run_id}.csv"
    )

    blob.write(output_path, result.output)

    manifest = {
        "run_id": run_id,
        "command": "daily",
        "as_of": request.as_of,
        "output": output_path,
        "input_hashes": result.input_hashes,
        "created_at": now(),
    }

    blob.write_json(f"manifests/{run_id}.json", manifest)

    return {
        "run_id": run_id,
        "output_file": output_path,
        "download_link": create_secure_link(output_path),
    }
```

## Teams Agent Behavior

The agent instructions should say:

```text
You are an ALC operations assistant.

You may:
- read current assets and rates
- run calculations
- create dated output files
- propose asset and rate changes

You must:
- show the current value before proposing a change
- show the proposed new value
- request explicit approval before changing assets or rates
- use the approved API endpoint for input changes
- never write directly to Azure Blob Storage
- never overwrite an existing output
- return links to dated output copies

For V1:
- invoice and month-end outputs may be generated without an approval gate
- do not claim that output posting is approval-controlled
- input changes always require authenticated approval
```

## Delivery Sequence

```text
1. Create Azure Storage account and container.
2. Upload assets.csv and rates.csv under inputs/.
3. Create outputs/, working/, manifests/, and archives/ prefixes.
4. Package ALC_1 as the engine.
5. Build FastAPI endpoints for daily, invoice, one-pager, and bank payable.
6. Add Blob download/upload handling.
7. Add dated output naming.
8. Add input proposal and approval endpoints.
9. Connect approval through Power Automate or Teams.
10. Connect Copilot Studio to the API.
11. Test normal runs and approved input changes.
12. Leave output approval controls for V2.
```

The core rule for V1 is:

> The agent can generate outputs freely, but it cannot change `assets.csv` or `rates.csv` without an authenticated user approval. Original inputs remain protected, and every generated result is a new dated copy.

[user.message]
I am lost in something. I did not see wrapers for invoices, posted invoices, bankpayable etc?

[assistant.message]
I’ll verify the `ALC_1/scripts` folder and map each wrapper to its engine command and generated files.

[assistant.message]
They are present in [ALC_1/scripts](ALC_1/scripts), but not every output has its own wrapper.

| Wrapper | Engine command | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `op_daily.sh` | `daily` | Daily report |
| `op_invoice.sh` | `invoice` | Creates invoice output and updates `posted_invoices.csv` |
| `op_bank_payable.sh` | `bank-payable` | Creates/updates `bank_payable.csv` |
| `op_month_end.sh` | `bank-payable` + `close-period` | Runs bank payable and closes the month |
| `op_one_pager.sh` | `one-pager` | Generates the Excel workbook |
| `op_schedule.sh` | `schedule` | Generates an amortization schedule |
| `op_init_baseline.sh` | `init-baseline` | Initializes a fresh baseline |

There is no separate `posted_invoices.sh` because `posted_invoices.csv` is an output ledger created and updated by the invoice command:

```text
op_invoice.sh
    -> calculates invoices
    -> writes invoice CSV if requested
    -> updates posted_invoices.csv
    -> refreshes the workbook
```

Likewise, `bank_payable.csv` is managed by:

```text
op_bank_payable.sh
```

or as part of:

```text
op_month_end.sh
```

The fresh `ALC_1` folder currently has no ledgers or outputs. They will be created when these wrappers are run.

```text
---------------|------------
```

```text
---------|----------|-------------|-------------|------------|-------
```

```text
Total asset value: $32,450,000
Active: 119
Inactive: 6
```
