2. Assets for a specific property
3. One asset by ID
```

The important controls are:

- read-only access
- source file name shown
- retrieval date shown
- no direct file editing through the display request
- no raw Azure path exposed to the user
- filtering by property, asset ID, status, or effective date

So the intended flow is:

```text
User asks in Teams
    -> Copilot calls read-only API
    -> API reads assets.csv or rates.csv
    -> API returns structured JSON
    -> Copilot formats it as a table or summary
```

The display functionality should be added to the Azure API. It should not require users to open Azure Blob Storage or inspect CSV files manually.

[user.message]

Yes, with one important distinction:

- **Showing current assets or rates** only reads the Blob files.
- **Adding or changing assets or rates** creates a proposal first.
- The Blob files are updated only after explicit authenticated approval.

Example:

```text
User: Add asset A-104 with value $250,000.
Agent: Proposed new asset. Approve?
User: Approve.
API: Validates approval and writes the updated assets.csv to Blob Storage.
```

Or:

Agent: Current rate is 6.6220%. Proposed value is 6.6171%. Approve?
User: Approve.
API: Archives the current rates.csv and writes the approved version.
```

The controlled flow is:

```text
Copilot
  -> reads current Blob input
  -> proposes add/change
  -> user approves in Teams
  -> API verifies approval
  -> API rechecks the file has not changed
  -> API archives the old file
  -> API writes the new assets.csv or rates.csv
  -> API records an audit manifest
```

The agent never receives direct write permission to Blob Storage. It requests the API to perform the approved update. This applies to both:

```text
assets.csv
rates.csv
```

Generated outputs remain separate and dated under `outputs/`. Output approval controls are reserved for Version 2.

## V1 Execution Flow

```text
User asks in Teams
    -> Copilot identifies month = 2026-08
    -> Agent calls POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
    -> API reads assets.csv and rates.csv from Blob
    -> API copies inputs into working/<run_id>/
    -> Python engine calculates the invoice
    -> API writes dated outputs
    -> API returns totals and links
    -> Copilot displays the result in Teams
```

Example API request:

```json
{
  "month": "2026-08",
  "billing_day": 31,
  "operator": "ana"
}
```

Equivalent engine call inside Azure:

```bash
python alc_item_sheet_tracker.py invoice \
  --operator ana \
  --month 2026-08 \
  --billing-day 31 \
  --output outputs/2026/08/invoices_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
```

## About `posted_invoices.csv`

The invoice engine needs a posted ledger so it knows which invoice rows have already been posted. In Azure, that ledger should live in a controlled operational location, for example:

```text
state/posted_invoices.csv
```

The API can also publish a dated copy:

```text
outputs/2026/08/invoices_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/posted-invoices-after-run_<run_id>.csv
```

For V1, the invoice call may update the operational ledger automatically because output approval controls are deferred. However:

- it must not modify `assets.csv`
- it must not modify `rates.csv`
- it must not overwrite the original input files
- it must create dated output copies
- it must record the run manifest

In V2, invoice posting itself can become:

```text
invoice preview
    -> user approval
    -> invoice commit
```

So the user experience in V1 is simply:

```text
User: Invoice this month.
Agent: August invoice completed. Here are the dated files and totals.
```

## API Response

The API could return:

```json
{
  "run_id": "20260821T143500-a81f",
  "status": "completed",
  "summary": {
    "month": "2026-08",
    "invoice_lines": 23,
    "invoice_total": 1270.58,
    "billing_date": "2026-08-31"
  },
  "files": [
    {
      "name": "invoices_2026-08-a81f.csv",
      "type": "invoice",
      "url": "secure-file-link"
    },
    {
      "name": "one-pager_2026-08-21-a81f.xlsx",
      "type": "workbook",
      "url": "secure-file-link"
    }
  ]
}
```

Copilot uses that response to compose the Teams message.

## Important Security Rule

The agent should receive only:

- file name
- summary
- secure link
- expiration information

It should not receive unrestricted Blob credentials or storage keys.

For V1, I recommend:

```text
Blob Storage = engine and output storage
SharePoint = user-facing report library
Teams = conversational delivery surface
```

That lets the user ask, “Invoice this month,” and receive dated, clickable files directly in Teams.

[user.message]
explain summary in agent receibe rule

[assistant.message]
The **agent receive rule** means the Copilot agent receives only the information needed to answer the user, not unrestricted access to Azure.

For an invoice run, the API returns:

```json
{
  "status": "completed",
  "run_id": "20260821-143500-a81f",
  "summary": {
    "month": "2026-08",
    "invoice_lines": 23,
    "invoice_total": 1270.58,
    "billing_date": "2026-08-31"
  },
  "files": [
    {
      "name": "invoices_2026-08-a81f.csv",
      "url": "secure-download-link"
    }
  ]
}
```

The agent receives:

- whether the operation succeeded
- the run ID
- important totals and dates
- file names
- secure links to the generated files

The agent does **not** receive:

- Azure storage account keys
- Blob connection strings
- unrestricted write credentials
- direct permission to replace `assets.csv` or `rates.csv`
- private internal storage paths

It then turns the API response into a Teams message:

```text
August 2026 invoice completed.

Invoice lines: 23
Invoice total: $1,270.58
Billing date: August 31, 2026

Download:
[Invoice CSV]
```

The secure link lets the user download the generated file, while the agent still cannot directly alter the protected source inputs.

## Phase 2: Create Azure Storage

```text
alc/
  inputs/
    assets.csv

  write inputs/
  write archives/
```

The original inputs remain protected.

## Phase 3: Package the Engine

Move the `ALC_1` engine into an Azure service package.

The API should call the existing Python engine rather than duplicate its financial logic.

```text
Azure API
    -> downloads inputs to working/<run_id>/
    -> runs Python engine
    -> uploads generated files
    -> returns summary and secure links
```

## Phase 4: Build the API

Initial read endpoints:

```text
GET /api/v1/assets
GET /api/v1/rates
```

Initial calculation endpoints:

```text
POST /api/v1/runs/daily
POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager
POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable
```

Input-change endpoints:

```text
POST /api/v1/assets/propose
POST /api/v1/rates/propose
POST /api/v1/assets/approve
POST /api/v1/rates/approve
```

## Phase 5: Generate Dated Copies

Every run creates new files:

```text
outputs/2026/08/21/daily_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/21/invoices_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
outputs/2026/08/21/one-pager_2026-08-21_<run_id>.xlsx
outputs/2026/08/21/bank-payable_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
```

No previous output is overwritten.

The operational ledgers remain in `state/`, while dated copies are published under `outputs/`.

## Phase 6: Return a Controlled API Response

The API returns:

```json
{
  "status": "completed",
  "run_id": "20260821-143500-a81f",
  "summary": {
    "month": "2026-08",
    "invoice_lines": 23,
    "invoice_total": 1270.58,
    "billing_date": "2026-08-31"
  },
  "files": [
    {
      "name": "invoices_2026-08-a81f.csv",
      "url": "secure-download-link"
    }
  ]
}
```

The response contains no Azure credentials or unrestricted storage access.

## Phase 7: Connect Copilot and Teams

Example user request:

```text
Invoice this month.
```

Agent flow:

```text
Copilot identifies the month
    -> calls invoice API
    -> receives summary and file links
    -> presents totals in Teams
    -> presents clickable dated files
```

Example response:

```text
August 2026 invoice completed.

Invoice lines: 23
Invoice total: $1,270.58
Billing date: August 31, 2026

[Download invoice CSV]
[Download workbook]
```

## Phase 8: Add Input Guardrails

For asset or rate changes:

```text
User requests change
    -> agent reads current value
    -> agent shows old and proposed values
    -> user approves in Teams
    -> API verifies authenticated approval
    -> API checks input hash
    -> API archives old file
    -> API writes new input file
    -> API records audit manifest
```

The agent never directly writes to Blob Storage.

## Phase 9: User Testing

Test these conversations:

```text
Show current rates.
Show assets for property P-100.
Run today's daily report.
Invoice this month.
Generate the one-pager.
Change the July rate.
Add a new asset.
Reject a proposed rate change.
Attempt an unauthorized input change.
```

Verify:

- summaries are correct
- dated files are delivered
- old outputs remain available
- inputs are protected
- approvals are recorded
- unauthorized changes fail

## Version 1 Boundary

V1 allows:

- read current inputs
- run calculations
- update assets and rates after approval
- generate operational outputs
- deliver dated files through Teams

V1 does not yet require approval for:

- invoice posting
- bank payable updates
- closing periods
- workbook publication

## Version 2

After V1 works reliably, add:

```text
invoice preview -> approval -> commit
month-end preview -> approval -> commit
bank payable preview -> approval -> commit
period close preview -> approval -> commit
```

This keeps the first Azure release focused: protect the source inputs, run the proven engine, create dated outputs, and deliver them clearly through Teams.

[user.message]
do we need to code something modifying the scripts or anything on ALC_1 now in order to follow that path?

[assistant.message]
Yes, but the changes should be made in `ALC_1`, not in the original `ALC` folder.

The existing financial engine can remain mostly unchanged. The Azure path needs a new layer around it.

## What does not need changing now

The following can remain as they are:

- financial calculations
- rate logic
- date logic
- asset schedule logic
- invoice logic
- existing local shell wrappers

The Azure service does not need to use the `.sh` wrappers. It can call Python directly.

## What needs to be added to `ALC_1`

```text
ALC_1/
  alc_item_sheet_tracker.py
  assets.csv
  rates.csv
  scripts/
  manuals/

  api/
    main.py
    models.py
    engine_runner.py
    storage.py
    approvals.py

  tests/
```

### 1. Azure storage adapter

`storage.py` would:

```python
download_blob("inputs/assets.csv", working_dir / "assets.csv")
download_blob("inputs/rates.csv", working_dir / "rates.csv")
upload_file(output_file, dated_output_path)
write_manifest(...)
```

This keeps Azure Blob logic out of the financial engine.

### 2. Engine runner

`engine_runner.py` would call the existing engine with explicit paths:

```python
run_engine(
    command="invoice",
    assets=working_dir / "assets.csv",
    rates=working_dir / "rates.csv",
    posted_ledger=working_dir / "posted_invoices.csv",
    output=working_dir / "invoices_2026-08-run123.csv",
)
```

The API controls which files the engine can see and where generated files are written.

### 3. Read endpoints

```text
GET /api/v1/assets
GET /api/v1/rates
```

These read the protected Blob inputs and return structured data to Copilot.

### 4. Run endpoints

```text
POST /api/v1/runs/daily
POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager
POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable
```

These create dated output copies and return summaries plus secure links.

### 5. Input approval endpoints

```text
POST /api/v1/assets/propose
POST /api/v1/rates/propose
POST /api/v1/assets/commit
POST /api/v1/rates/commit
```

These are what prevent the agent from directly changing `assets.csv` or `rates.csv`.

## Important design decision

The API should run the engine with isolated paths:

```text
Azure inputs
    -> temporary working folder
    -> Python engine
    -> temporary generated files
    -> dated Azure outputs
```

The engine should never receive direct write access to:

```text
inputs/assets.csv
inputs/rates.csv
```

## First coding step

The first implementation step should be:

```text
Create api/storage.py
Create api/engine_runner.py
Create api/main.py
```

Then test one endpoint locally:

```text
POST /api/v1/runs/daily
```

Only after that works should we add:

- invoice endpoint
- one-pager endpoint
- asset/rate proposal flow
- Teams/Copilot connection

So the answer is:

> We do not need to rewrite the existing scripts. We need to add an Azure/API layer inside `ALC_1` that controls paths, storage, dated outputs, and approved input changes.

[user.message]
why the engine runner does not have banks, for instance?

[assistant.message]
It should support bank payable. I used `engine_runner` as a generic name, not as an invoice-only component.

The runner should expose all existing engine commands:

```python
run_engine("daily", ...)
run_engine("invoice", ...)
run_engine("bank-payable", ...)
run_engine("one-pager", ...)
run_engine("schedule", ...)
```

The API would have separate user-facing endpoints:

```text
POST /api/v1/runs/daily
POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable
POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager
```

For bank payable:

```text
User: Calculate bank payable for August.
    -> Copilot calls POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable
    -> API downloads assets.csv and rates.csv
    -> API downloads the operational ledger if needed
    -> engine runs bank-payable
    -> API uploads a dated bank-payable output
    -> API returns summary and file link
```

Equivalent engine call:

```bash
python alc_item_sheet_tracker.py bank-payable \
  --operator ana \
  --month 2026-08 \
  --billing-day 31 \
  --bank-payable-file working/<run_id>/bank_payable.csv
```

The output could be published as:

```text
outputs/2026/08/bank-payable_2026-08_<run_id>.csv
```

The generic runner would map the command to the correct files:

```python
COMMANDS = {
    "daily": run_daily,
    "invoice": run_invoice,
    "bank-payable": run_bank_payable,
    "one-pager": run_one_pager,
    "schedule": run_schedule,
}
```

So the architecture includes banks; it is just not represented by a separate file called `bank_runner.py`.

A clearer structure could be:

```text
api/
  main.py
  storage.py
  engine_runner.py
  daily_service.py
  invoice_service.py
  bank_payable_service.py
  one_pager_service.py
```

`engine_runner.py` handles common execution. `bank_payable_service.py` handles the bank-specific request, file naming, summary, and API response.

[user.message]
so those are examples, not all, right:

[user.message]
so those are examples, not all, right: 
run_engine("daily", ...)
run_engine("invoice", ...)
run_engine("bank-payable", ...)
run_engine("one-pager", ...)
run_engine("schedule", ...)

[assistant.message]
Correct. Those are examples of the engine commands, not necessarily the complete final API design.

The current engine supports these commands:

```text
snapshot
invoice
bank-payable
close-period
daily
schedule
init-baseline
one-pager
```

The runner should support all of them:

```python
run_engine("snapshot", ...)
run_engine("invoice", ...)
run_engine("bank-payable", ...)
run_engine("close-period", ...)
run_engine("daily", ...)
run_engine("schedule", ...)
run_engine("init-baseline", ...)
run_engine("one-pager", ...)
```

But they should not all be exposed identically to Copilot.

## Read-only or reporting commands

These can be exposed as normal operations:

```text
snapshot
daily
schedule
one-pager
```

## Operational commands

These create or update operational state:

```text
invoice
bank-payable
close-period
init-baseline
```

For V1, invoice and bank payable can run through the API and create dated output copies. `close-period` and `init-baseline` should probably be restricted to administrator-only endpoints because they change control state.

A better API mapping is:

```text
GET  /api/v1/assets
GET  /api/v1/rates

POST /api/v1/runs/snapshot
POST /api/v1/runs/daily
POST /api/v1/runs/schedule
POST /api/v1/runs/one-pager
POST /api/v1/runs/invoice
POST /api/v1/runs/bank-payable

POST /api/v1/admin/close-period
POST /api/v1/admin/init-baseline

POST /api/v1/assets/propose
POST /api/v1/rates/propose
POST /api/v1/assets/commit
POST /api/v1/rates/commit
```

So:

- the **engine runner** should be generic and support the complete engine command set
- the **API** should expose only the operations appropriate for each user role
- Copilot should not be allowed to invoke administrative commands by default
- input changes remain approval-controlled
- output approval remains a V2 enhancement as planned

[user.message]
what does one pager do?

[assistant.message]
The **one-pager** creates or refreshes an Excel workbook with a separate worksheet for each asset.

It combines:

- asset information
- property and allocation
- asset cost
- lease terms
- start and maturity dates
- applicable bank rate
- monthly lease rate
- payment amount
- interest
- amortization
- remaining lease balance
- bank funding balance
- lifecycle status

The workbook also includes an inventory-level view that compares lease calculations with bank payable information.

The command is:

```bash
python alc_item_sheet_tracker.py one-pager --operator ana
```

It creates or refreshes:

```text
ALC - Asset Calculation Unit.xlsx
```

In Azure V1, the workbook should not overwrite a shared original. It should be generated as a dated copy, for example:

```text
outputs/2026/08/21/one-pager_2026-08-21_<run_id>.xlsx
```

So when the user says:

```text
Create the one-pager.
```

the agent runs the engine, uploads the dated workbook, and returns a secure Teams link to it.

## Phase 9: Apply Azure Permissions

70. Normal API identity:
   - read `inputs/`
   - read/write `working/`
   - read/write `state/`
   - write `outputs/`
   - write `manifests/`
   - read/write `archives/`
   - no write access to `inputs/`

71. Input-commit identity:
   - read `inputs/`
   - write `inputs/`
   - write `archives/`
   - write `manifests/`

72. Enable Blob versioning and soft delete.
73. Do not give the Copilot agent storage keys.
74. Require the API to enforce all writes.

The snapshot route creates a point-in-time portfolio report without invoicing or changing the input files.

## User request

```text
Show me the asset snapshot as of August 21, 2026.
```

## API call

```http
POST /api/v1/runs/snapshot
```

Request:

```json
{
  "operator": "ana",
  "as_of": "2026-08-21"
}
```

## What happens

```text
Copilot
  -> API receives snapshot date
  -> API reads assets.csv and rates.csv
  -> API copies them into working/<run_id>/
  -> engine runs snapshot --as-of 2026-08-21
  -> snapshot CSV is created in that run folder
  -> API returns summary and file metadata
  -> Copilot presents the result in Teams
```

The underlying engine command is:

```bash
python alc_item_sheet_tracker.py snapshot \
  --operator ana \
  --as-of 2026-08-21 \
  --assets working/<run_id>/assets.csv \
  --rates working/<run_id>/rates.csv \
  --output working/<run_id>/snapshot.csv
```

## What the snapshot contains

For each asset, it reports information such as:

- asset ID
- property and allocation
- asset value
- start and maturity dates
- lifecycle status
- lease balance
- lease payment
- interest
- amortization
- bank balance
- bank payment
- applicable rates

It is a calculation as of the requested date, not a ledger-posting operation.

## Example response

```json
{
  "status": "completed",
  "run_id": "abc123",
  "command": "snapshot",
  "as_of": "2026-08-21",
  "files": [
    {
      "name": "snapshot.csv",
      "path": "working/abc123/snapshot.csv"
    }
  ]
}
```

In the final Azure version, that file would be copied to:

```text
outputs/2026/08/21/snapshot_abc123.csv
```

and Copilot would return a secure Teams download link.

The snapshot route is read/calculation-only:

- it does not update `assets.csv`
- it does not update `rates.csv`
- it does not post invoices
- it does not close a period
- it does not update bank payable state

[user.message]
but it gives a snapshot of everything there is on rates or assets, right?

[assistant.message]
Almost.

The snapshot route gives the **portfolio state of every asset as of a selected date**. It uses the applicable rate for each asset’s calculation, but it does **not** produce a complete history or listing of all rates.

### Snapshot includes

For every asset:

- asset ID and property
- asset value
- status
- lease balance
- payment
- interest
- amortization
- bank balance
- applicable bank rate and lease rate
- start and maturity dates

### Snapshot does not include

- every historical rate in `rates.csv`
- a complete rates table
- every change ever made to assets
- audit history

For those requests, the agent uses the read endpoints:

```text
GET /api/v1/assets
GET /api/v1/rates
```

So:

```text
"Give me the asset position as of August 21"
    -> snapshot
```

```text
"Show me all current recorded assets"
    -> assets endpoint
```

```text
"Show me all recorded rates"
    -> rates endpoint
```

The snapshot is the calculated **current portfolio position**, not a dump of all source records.

[user.message]
ah! it works basically on each tab of the workbook but the first one with is the summary?

[assistant.message]
Exactly.

The one-pager workbook contains:

1. A first summary tab, likely **Inventory**, with the overall portfolio view.
2. One tab per asset, containing the detailed calculation for that asset.

The summary tab shows the portfolio-level information, such as:

- all assets
- property and allocation
- asset value
- lease balances
- bank balances
- payment totals
- interest and amortization
- lifecycle status

The individual tabs provide the detailed asset-level schedule and calculations.

So the workflow is:

```text
one-pager workbook
  -> first tab: portfolio summary
  -> remaining tabs: one tab per asset
```

The snapshot CSV is different: it is a tabular export of the asset-level point-in-time data, without the workbook’s multi-tab presentation.

For an invoice run in `ALC_1`, the engine creates several files.

## Invoice files created

### Operational ledger

```text
ALC_1/state/posted_invoices.csv
```

This is the working historical record. It contains the invoice rows already posted and is used by future calculations to avoid treating them as unposted.

### Dated invoice output

```text
ALC_1/outputs/YYYY/MM/DD/invoices_<month>_<run_id>.csv
```

This is the user-facing copy for that particular run.

### Dated workbook

```text
ALC_1/outputs/YYYY/MM/DD/one-pager_<month>_<run_id>.xlsx
```

The invoice command also refreshes the one-pager workbook.

### Run manifest

```text
ALC_1/manifests/<run_id>.json
```

This records the run, operator, month, input hashes, totals, and generated artifacts.

### Backups

```text
ALC_1/archives/
```

If an existing state or output file was replaced, the previous version is backed up there.

## How the user views them

In Azure V1, the user would say:

```text

The agent presents it in Teams:

[assistant.message]
That is the next safety step: **approval/commit**.

A proposal only records a requested change. It does not modify the inputs.

The commit step would:

1. Receive the proposal ID and approval.
2. Confirm the approver is authorized.
3. Check the proposal has not expired.
4. Verify `assets.csv` or `rates.csv` has not changed since the proposal.
5. Archive the current file.
6. Apply the approved change.
7. Record who approved it and when.

Example:

```text
User: Change July rate to 6.70%.
Agent: Proposed change. Approve?
User: Approve.
API: Updates rates.csv and records the audit.
```

Without approval, the proposal remains:

```text
pending_approval
```

and the source files stay unchanged.

[user.message]
will this run generate big costs on azure tenant consumption due to size and machine capacity using this scripts?

[assistant.message]
I’ll assess the likely Azure consumption profile from the current engine design, separating storage, compute, and hosting choices.No. With the current ALC files and script workload, consumption should be low.

The engine processes:

- small CSV input files
- a relatively small asset portfolio
- standard Python calculations
- occasional CSV and Excel output generation

It should not require a large Azure machine.

## Main cost areas

### Blob Storage

Expected to be very low:

- small input files
- small ledgers
- dated CSVs and workbooks
- manifests and archives

The biggest growth factor would be retaining every generated output indefinitely. A lifecycle policy can move or delete old files after a defined period.

### Compute

Use a serverless or scale-to-zero option:

```text
Azure Functions
```

or:

```text
Azure Container Apps with scale-to-zero
```

The API runs only when a user requests a report or change. There should be no continuously running server cost.

### API hosting

Avoid an always-on virtual machine for V1. A VM would create unnecessary cost even when nobody uses the agent.

Recommended initial model:

```text
Copilot Studio
    -> Power Automate
    -> Azure API
    -> Python engine
    -> Blob Storage
```

with consumption-based compute.

## One-pager consideration

The one-pager uses `openpyxl` and may use more memory than a CSV-only report, but with the current portfolio it should still be modest. We should test the real workbook generation in Azure-like conditions before selecting a very small memory tier.

## Cost controls

Request these from the Azure administrator:

- scale-to-zero compute
- consumption-based billing
- storage lifecycle rules
- budget alert
- monthly cost alert
- application logging with retention limits
- no always-on VM
- no unnecessary premium storage tier
- no unnecessary high-memory hosting plan

## Practical conclusion

The current engine is not large enough to create significant Azure consumption by itself. The main architectural cost risk would be choosing an always-running VM or premium hosting service, not the Python calculations.

For V1, the cost-conscious choice is:

```text
Private Blob Storage
+ Azure Functions or scale-to-zero Container Apps
+ Managed Identity
+ consumption budget alert
```

The tenant administrator should confirm the exact pricing for the organization’s Azure region and subscription, but the expected workload is small.

```md
# ALC Azure V1 Enablement SOW

**Owner:** German Montoya  
**User:** gmontoya@acentopartners.com  
**Status:** Azure tenant enablement request

## Objective

Make the validated ALC Python engine available through Microsoft Teams and Copilot Studio. Users should be able to request calculations and receive dated CSV/XLSX outputs without running Python or shell scripts.

Asset and rate inputs must remain protected. Changes to `assets.csv` or `rates.csv` require explicit authenticated user approval.

## Current State

The validated engine is in:

```text
ALC_1/alc_item_sheet_tracker.py
```

It already supports:

- daily reports
- snapshots
- schedules
- invoice calculations
- posted-invoice tracking
- bank-payable calculations
- one-pager workbook generation
- validated date, rate, and asset behavior

A local FastAPI API boundary has been started in `ALC_1/api/`.

## Azure V1 Architecture

```text
Copilot Studio / Teams
        |
   Power Automate
        |
   Authenticated API
        |
   ALC Python engine
        |
   Azure Blob Storage
```

## Blob Storage Structure

```text
inputs/assets.csv
inputs/rates.csv

state/posted_invoices.csv
state/bank_payable.csv
state/closed_periods.csv

working/<run_id>/

outputs/YYYY/MM/DD/

manifests/
archives/
```

Original input files remain protected. Every generated report is saved as a new dated copy with a unique run ID.

## API Capabilities

The API will allow users to:

- view current assets and rates
- run daily, snapshot, schedule, invoice, bank-payable, and one-pager operations
- view operational history and run manifests
- receive calculation summaries
- receive secure links to dated CSV/XLSX outputs
- propose new or changed assets and rates
- commit asset/rate changes only after authenticated approval

The existing Python engine remains the single source of financial truth. Financial calculations will not be rewritten in Copilot, Power Automate, or the API.

## V1 Guardrails

- The normal agent/API identity can read `inputs/` but cannot write it.
- The Copilot agent cannot write directly to Blob Storage.
- Asset and rate changes require:
  - a proposal
  - explicit user approval
  - input-hash verification
  - archival of the prior file
  - an audit manifest
- Generated outputs are dated and are never overwritten.
- Approval controls for invoice posting, bank-payable updates, period closing, and final workbook publication are deferred to V2.

## Requested Azure Enablement

Please provision or enable:

1. A resource group and private Azure Storage Account.
2. A private Blob container for the ALC paths above.
3. Azure Functions or scale-to-zero Azure Container Apps for the API and Python runtime.
4. A managed identity for the API runtime.
5. Entra ID authentication for the API.
6. API access for:

```text
gmontoya@acentopartners.com
```

7. Runtime permissions to:
   - read `inputs/`
   - read/write `working/`
   - read/write operational `state/`
   - write `outputs/`
   - write `manifests/`
   - write `archives/`
   - not write `inputs/`

8. A separate controlled permission path for approved writes to `assets.csv` and `rates.csv`.
9. Required networking, configuration, secrets, logging, and monitoring.
10. Blob versioning, soft delete, and lifecycle retention.
11. A consumption budget and cost alert.

## Existing Microsoft 365 Access

The Microsoft 365 environment is already available to the owner.

The owner can already:

- create and deploy Copilot Studio agents
- create and connect Power Automate flows
- use the existing Microsoft 365 environment

No additional Copilot Studio or Power Automate environment permission is requested for this SOW.

## V1 Acceptance Criteria

- Users can ask Copilot for current assets and rates.
- Users can request supported ALC calculations.
- The API runs the existing engine without duplicating financial logic.
- Outputs are delivered as dated, secure Teams-accessible files.
- The normal agent/API identity cannot modify the original input files.
- Approved asset/rate changes are archived and auditable.
- Stale proposals are rejected if the inputs changed.
- Azure hosting uses consumption-based or scale-to-zero resources.

## Deferred to V2

- approval before invoice posting
- approval before bank-payable updates
- approval before closing periods
- approval before final workbook publication
- expanded dashboards and analytics
```

Yes, we should address both now. The SOW has been updated:

It now states:

- **Security boundary:** no subscription-wide administration; access should be limited to the approved resource group/application resources.

The important distinction is:

```text
Runtime identity = runs the API
Deployment identity = publishes new API versions
User identity = accesses the API and manages the integration
```

You should request deployment rights explicitly. Otherwise, the administrator may provision the resources but you may be unable to deploy updates afterward.

to create the first version, do we need all those identities?

[assistant.message]
No. For the first version, we can keep it to **three practical identities**:

1. **Your user identity**
   - `gmontoya@acentopartners.com`
   - Calls the API and manages Copilot/Power Automate.
   - Receives approval requests.

2. **API runtime managed identity**
   - Runs the Python engine.
   - Reads inputs.
   - Writes outputs, state, manifests, and archives.
   - Must not write protected inputs directly.

3. **Deployment identity**
   - Used by you or CI/CD to deploy API updates.
   - Can be your user with narrowly scoped resource-group deployment permission.
   - A separate CI/CD identity can be added later.

A separate approval identity is **not required** for V1. The approval can come from your authenticated Microsoft 365 identity through Power Automate, and the API verifies the approval before committing an input change.

However, there is an important Azure Blob detail: folders such as `inputs/` and `outputs/` are virtual paths, not independent security boundaries. For strong permissions, use separate containers:

```text
alc-inputs       protected assets.csv and rates.csv
alc-state        posted ledgers and operational state
alc-outputs      dated generated files
alc-manifests    proposals and audit records
alc-archives     archived input versions
```

For V1, the minimum request is:

- your Entra ID access
- one runtime managed identity
- scoped deployment access for you or CI/CD
- separate protected input storage
- approval enforced by the API

We can add a separate commit identity later if the security team requires stronger separation.

[assistant.message]
I’ll outline the ALC cloud/API pseudocode in a clear, implementation-ready form so you can use it immediately.## ALC Cloud + API pseudocode

Here is a clean, implementation-ready pseudocode model for an ALC system running in the cloud and exposed through an API.

### 1) High-level cloud flow

```python
# ALC Cloud application flow

class ALCCloudApp:
    def __init__(self):
        self.db = connect_database()
        self.cache = connect_cache()
        self.queue = connect_message_queue()
        self.storage = connect_object_storage()
        self.auth = init_auth_service()
        self.logger = init_logger()

    def startup(self):
        self.logger.info("Starting ALC Cloud service")
        self.load_configuration()
        self.connect_dependencies()
        self.start_background_workers()
        self.start_api_server()

    def start_background_workers(self):
        schedule_task("sync_data_from_sources")
        schedule_task("reconcile_records")
        schedule_task("generate_reports")
        schedule_task("cleanup_old_logs")

    def sync_data_from_sources(self):
        sources = get_registered_sources()
        for source in sources:
            raw_data = fetch_data_from_source(source)
            validated = validate_data(raw_data)
            save_to_database(validated)
            publish_event("data.synced", {"source": source.name})

    def reconcile_records(self):
        records = get_pending_reconciliations()
        for record in records:
            matched = match_record(record)
            if matched:
                update_status(record, "reconciled")
            else:
                flag_for_review(record)

    def generate_reports(self):
        report_data = build_summary_metrics()
        file_url = store_report(report_data)
        notify_users(file_url)
```

---

### 2) API layer pseudocode

```python
# API request flow

class ALCAPI:
    def __init__(self, app_service):
        self.app_service = app_service

    def handle_request(self, request):
        try:
            auth_result = self.authenticate(request.headers)
            if not auth_result.valid:
                return response(401, {"error": "Unauthorized"})

            route = parse_route(request.path)
            method = request.method

            if route == "/login" and method == "POST":
                return self.login(request.body)

            if route == "/alc/items" and method == "GET":
                return self.get_items(request.query, auth_result.user)

            if route == "/alc/items" and method == "POST":
                return self.create_item(request.body, auth_result.user)

            if route == "/alc/items/{id}" and method == "GET":
                return self.get_item_by_id(request.path.id, auth_result.user)

            if route == "/alc/items/{id}" and method == "PUT":
                return self.update_item(request.path.id, request.body, auth_result.user)

            if route == "/alc/items/{id}" and method == "DELETE":
                return self.delete_item(request.path.id, auth_result.user)

            return response(404, {"error": "Route not found"})
        except ValidationError as e:
            return response(400, {"error": str(e)})
        except Exception as e:
            self.logger.exception("Unhandled API error")
            return response(500, {"error": "Internal server error"})
```

---

### 3) Service-layer pseudocode

```python
class ALCService:
    def login(self, username, password):
        user = db.find_user_by_username(username)
        if not user:
            raise ValidationError("User not found")

        if not verify_password(password, user.password_hash):
            raise ValidationError("Invalid credentials")

        token = create_jwt(user.id)
        return {"token": token, "user": user.public_profile()}

    def get_items(self, filters, user):
        authorize_user(user, "read:alc_items")
        items = db.query_items(filters)
        return {"items": items, "count": len(items)}

    def create_item(self, payload, user):
        authorize_user(user, "write:alc_items")
        validate_payload(payload)

        item = {
            "id": generate_uuid(),
            "name": payload["name"],
            "status": "pending",
            "created_by": user.id,
            "created_at": now()
        }

        db.insert_item(item)
        publish_event("alc.item.created", {"item_id": item["id"]})
        return response(201, item)

    def update_item(self, item_id, payload, user):
        authorize_user(user, "write:alc_items")
        existing = db.get_item(item_id)
        if not existing:
            raise ValidationError("Item not found")

        updated = merge(existing, payload)
        validate_updated_data(updated)
        db.update_item(item_id, updated)
        publish_event("alc.item.updated", {"item_id": item_id})
        return updated

    def delete_item(self, item_id, user):
        authorize_user(user, "delete:alc_items")
        db.soft_delete_item(item_id)
        publish_event("alc.item.deleted", {"item_id": item_id})
        return {"deleted": True}
```

---

### 4) Database + workflow pseudocode

```python
# Core persistence and business workflow

def process_new_item(item_payload):
    item = validate_item(item_payload)
    save_to_db(item)
    enqueue_task("calculate_item_metrics", item["id"])
    enqueue_task("notify_team", item["id"])
    return item

def calculate_item_metrics(item_id):
    item = db.get_item(item_id)
    metrics = {
        "total_cost": compute_total_cost(item),
        "status_score": compute_status_score(item),
        "risk_level": compute_risk_level(item)
    }
    db.update_item_metrics(item_id, metrics)
    publish_event("alc.metrics.updated", {"item_id": item_id})

def notify_team(item_id):
    item = db.get_item(item_id)
    if item["status"] == "critical":
        send_email_alert("team@company.com", f"ALC item {item_id} requires attention")
```

```python
# Typical cloud deployment architecture

def deploy_alc_cloud():
    create_kubernetes_cluster()
    deploy_api_service("alc-api")
    deploy_worker_service("alc-worker")
    deploy_database("postgres")
    deploy_cache("redis")
    deploy_queue("rabbitmq")
    configure_object_storage("s3-like-bucket")
    configure_monitoring()
    configure_security_policies()
    route_traffic_through_api_gateway()

### 6) End-to-end example

```python
# Example transaction flow

def handle_user_request_example():
    request = {
        "method": "POST",
        "path": "/alc/items",
        "headers": {"Authorization": "Bearer valid_token"},
        "body": {
            "name": "Office Chair",
            "category": "Furniture",
            "cost": 250
        }
    }

    response = ALCAPI(ALCService()).handle_request(request)

    if response.status == 201:
        print("Item created successfully")
    else:
        print(response.body)
```

---

### Summary

The pattern is:

- API receives request
- authenticates and validates
- calls service layer
- service validates business rules
- database persists data
- event/queue triggers background jobs
- workers generate reports, reconcile data, and notify downstream systems

- Flask/FastAPI code
- Node.js/Express code
- Azure cloud architecture
- AWS cloud architecture
